A BRILLIANT STORY

A POPULAR WRITER.

Arrangements have been made, readers will be interested to know, for the publica-

tion in our columns of a story of unusual interest entitled :

7Ty V RITT.EN IN SCARLET,"

ALICE MAUD MEADOWS.

AtiTIIOII OF,

"ONE LIFE BETWEEN."

"A MILLION OF MONEY."

"THE EXTREME PENALTY."

"FOR LOVE OF A LADY."

"I CHARGE YOU BOTH."

The name of MISS MEADOWS will be familiar to the most casual reader of news- paper fiction. No writer of serials is more popular throughout the country. She has a wonderful facility for the writing of sensa- tional stories, and, being one of the most industrious of literary women, has turned out an astonishing amount of work. She was for some years the editor of the "Princess's Novelettes," for which she wrote many of the stories herself, and was at the same time responsible for the corn- ps.nion weekly. "Something to Read," in which her own serials wore running. Her output is less, but the quality of her work higher, since she put off editorial cares. Her power of holding the attention of the reader will be found exemplified, we are sure, in the story we are about to publish.

4 6 WRITrENT /LC SCARLET,"

A LICE MAUD MEADOWS.

===== 0002601_19061110_art0007.txt | included=true | included =====
OUTLINE OF PLOT.

MISS MEADOWS writes of love and crime, and the rivalry of two brothers for a fair maiden's hand. John Brandon, a well-to-do country gentleman, is about to be married to Celia Verde. The young lady was once engaged to John's brother Dick, a wild and reckless fellow, who had gone abroad in search of adventure, and is supposed to have met his death. Celia admits that she loved the old sweetheart better than the new—in fact is doubtful whether she loves John at all. The infatuated lover promises himself, however, that hewill win her love after marriage. Suddenly all is changed by the return of Dick, alive and well. No one has recog- nised him except John, to whose hopes his appearance gives the death-blow. Dick laughs at the idea of Celia Verde marrying John, certain that she is true to her love for himself. The taunt excites John to fury, and in his library he re-enacts the tragedy of Cain, hiding the body in a large safe. Then a convict who has just escaped from prison appears on the scene, and. being aware of the crime, insists that Brandon shall shelter him by giving him clothes and keeping him in the house as one of the wedding guests, under the name of Vernon Salisbury. Fresh surprises come when the safe is

opened by the police, who believe the

- escaped convict to be concealed there, and is found empty. How this comes about Vernon Salisbury can explain, but he, too, is staggered by the disappearance of the body also from a place in the grounds where he had hidden it. Sensatlim, indeed, crowds upon sensation as the plot develops. The story will be followed to the end with the closest attention, the more so as to these sensational happenings is added the • attractions of a strong love interest.

4‘ . BITTEN IN ISCARIET!'

ALICE NEAuo MEADOWS

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

PRINCESS'S MAGIC WAND. • .

The latest story of Princess Victoria Luiso,l the Kaiser's only daughter, is that while stay- ing at the castle of Rominter she sent for ten little girls from the neighbouring village, made them form a ring round her, and, with a card- board wand in her hand, asked them a Geried of questions. At the end of the examinayon, the Empress, who was standing by, spoke up: "Now comes punishment for those who have answered wrongly," and the little Princess laughingly dragged forward one of the girls, made, her put out her hand, and—whack! came the end of the stick down on the tight-stretched little palm. But instead of stinging pain the culprit felt something hard left behind in her hand—a stick of chocolate. There was an outcry at once that all the answers had been wrong, and the wand continued to deal out its benevo- lent chastisements till the chocolate stuffing was exhausted. The Emperor has given his daughter the title of the "Village Qoverness."

Straight Hair Made Curly.—Strong healthy hair we are told, is straight and lank, and when it curls naturally it is only because it is of inferior quality, and full of flaws and defects. It is the breaks and perfora- tions in it that makes it wavy, and if you have straight hair, all you have to do to it, according to this latest theory, is to have these defects reproduced by an artificial process, when it will become naturally and permanently curl. The occupation of the professional "waver" will be gone, for, as naturally curly hair is never so wavy as on a wet day, so the straight locks made curly will, it is said, brave the wettest of Scotch mists. At first one is inclined to be a little sceptical about such promises, but it would be a boon to vast numbers of women, if they should prove in the course of time to be justified by practical results. The Millinery World.—Flowers, so say authorities on the subject, will be worn all through the winter, the only difference lying in the fact that they are built of velvet instead of lighter materials. A distinct novelty however, is the uncurled plumage of vulture, which is ornmenting the most up-to-date hats. The effect is much the same as in the case of uncurled ostrich feathers, namely stringy, and headgear so trimmed may be said to be curious and weird, rather than beautiful. About Colours.—The painfully violent early Victorian colours, which modistes endeavour to brine' forward at the commencement of the autumn, are disappearing, and the artistic woman is apparently to be spared many shocks that seemed to be so surely in store for her a few weeks back. Where the dyers of the middle of last century obtained their inspirations, it would be difficult to imagine, but they certainly did not go direct to Nature, as they do nowadays. With such exquisite hues as the dyers of to-thy place before us, it seems incomprehensible that fashionable dressmakers end milliners should demand colours that are hideously

Drowning Fatality at Warrington. An inquest was held at Warrington, on Tuesday, I by Mr. Brighouse, on the body of Walter Barber, I general labourer, who resided at 35, Welfield-street, Warrington. Deceased, who was 26 years of age, was : the son of Mr. Charles Bather, Bradley Hill Farm, I Lymm.—Mr. William H. Owen, secretary of Messrs.! Monks, Hall and Co., Ltd., said he went to where deceased and other men were working at Atherton's Quay about five o'clock on October 26th to try the oilskins,and instructed Barber to get a bucket of water oat of the river. Barber went down the quay side byl a ladder and on to a boat, and Mr. Owen was of the I opinion that in attempting to draw the water the 1 strong curtent running at the time pulled him in ' No one saw Barber fall into the water, but a splash I was heard. Directly afterwards he was seen struggling in the water, and a lifebuoy was' thrown to him. Barber failed to grasp it and sank. Grappling operations were carried on by the Runcorn Grappling Corps, under the direction of Captain Bvfield, hut the body was seen lying in the mud ..t Bank Quay on Sunday by a man named James David- son.—A verdict of "Accidental death " was returned. Mr. Steel attended the inquest in the interests of deceased's relatives.

Besides being terribly nervous and depressed and upset by trifles, my appetite, which bad been grow- ing smaller and smaller, left me. Then my stomach either rejected food in violent fits of vomiting, or else it lay on my chest undigested and gave me most agonizing pain. This happened until I TURNED FROM THE SIGHT OF FOOD. I had hardly enough strength to stand upright, while to go upstairs was almost beyond me. I would stop, struggling for breath, with my heart palpitating. My lips and cheeks became white as a sheet, and my eyes dark and hollow. So bloodless did I become that my flesh was almost transparent. Weary to death all day, and longing to sleep. I never seemed able to get rest at night, but woke up every, morning exhausted and miser- able.

Rev. H. Donnie° and National Decay. Preaching in Kensington Baptist church, Liverpool, on Sunday evening, the Rev. H. Dnnnico made specild reference to the causes of national decay. In the coure,e of his sermon he said that to him it was deplorable that men,whose Sole desire for governing the city was to protect a vested interest, should secure their ends on the hypocritical pretence of being anxious to preserve religious teaching in our schools. He yielded to no man in the desire to see sterling Christian men in the seats of authority ; but be would prefer to be ruled by such men as John Morley, John Burns, and Robert Blatch- ford, agnostics though they were, than by the " friends of the trade" returned to power with their aid and to protect their interests. The so-called " trade "did more to blight and curse human life in a year than Church-Catechisms did good in twenty.

A SAILOR'S MEAN THEFT.

At Runcorn Petty Sessions, on Monday, before Messrs. A. Thomas and John Littler, Alexande, Gallo- way, a young seaman, was charged with having stolen 155., the money of Robert Ross, 'from the steamer Sapphire, at Runcorn, on the 3rd or 4th inst.—Robert Ross, fireman on the steamer, which belongs to Messrs. Robinson and Sons, of Glasgow, said that at ten o'clock on Friday night, the steamer being in the docks at Man- chester, the prisoner came on board and said he was destitute, apd asked to be taken as far as Runcorn. He was allowell to remain, anc slept in the men's quarters. The boat came to Runcorn on Saturday, and prisoner again slept aboard. That night he (prosecutor) hung his trousers up in the sleeping quarters, and when he went to them next morning he missed abut 15s. The prisoner had gone. He gave information to the police. —P.C. Wilding said he received information and arrested prisoner in High-street on Sunday.—Prisoner said he did not steal so much. He pleaded guilty to having stolen a lesser amount.—The Chairman said it was very shabby of prisoner to steal the man's money after having been brought to Runcorn on the boat for nothing,— Prisoner: I could not get a ship and had nothing. I am very sorry.—The Chairman: You might have left the money alone anyway. You must go to prison for a month.

_ Rochdale H. Batley Oldham 6 3 21 Leigh 0 0 0 Iluncorn Warrington v Wigan. There was the biggest gate of the season at Warring- ton on Saturday, when Wigan were visitors, quite 6,000 spectators assembling. Warrington had to find sub- stitutes for Isherwood, Lees, and Naylor, while VVigart were handicapped by the absence of Jenkins and Leytham, who turned out for the county. Fine weather Prevailed, and a very keen, fast game was witnessed. Teams and officials : Warrington—Berry. back ; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor, and Brooks. three-quarter backs; Hockenhall and Jordan', half backs; Heath, Harmer, Thomas, Boardman, Sugars, and Preston, forwards. Wiean—Mason, back ; Treharne, Jones, Coulthard, and Green, three-quarter backs ; Thomas and Anderson, half backs ; Morris, Whittaker, Blears, Walton, Kaye. and Brooks. forwards. Referee. Mr. P. Clegg', Manningharn. Touch judges, Messrs. J. Roscoe, Broughton Rankers, and J. Lawton, Oldham. Wigan early pressed, and a fine effort by Treharme and Jones almost overcame Berry's sound defence. Fish drove the visitors out, and fast work between Hocken- hall and Jordan led up to Warrington attacking. Fish snapped ma the ball from Mason's toes, and making an Opening for Dickenson, that player crossed. Strong forward play gave Wigan the advantage, and Thomas and Anderson were held up just inside. Near the interval Fish in trying to save his lines, deliberately knocked the ball behind, and a penalty was awarded the visitors, from which Mason placed a goal. A brilliant run by Brooks was saved by the Wigan custodian, and at half- time Warrington led by three points to two. Turning round Wigan were early penalised, and Fish Placed his side further ahead, and shortly afterwards a second penalty was turned to good account by Fish. Wigan now had a prolonged spell of attacking, and repeatedly got to the line only to find Berry's defence very sound. A capital sprint by Fish led up to another attack, and Harmer scored from a cross-kick,and Preston added a goal. Wigan got well inside the home quarters in the last few minutes. and Green scored at the corner. 'Result :—Warrington 3 goals, 2 tries (12 points) ; Wigan, 1 goal, 1 try (5 points). RUNCORN v. SALFORD. At Runcorn, before 2,000 spectators. The start vas dflayed half an hour owing to the late arrival of the visitors. In the first two minutes the Runcorn forwards made a desperate rush to the Salford line, where the visitors were penalised, Richardson placing a goal. Brady, Preston, Gurnee, and Wallwork made headway with passing, but Moran dribbled to the Salford line, Ttla.ere Flanagan picked up and scored, Richardson fail- at att goal. Vigorous play followed, and Richardson further two points from a penalty kick. Hump- son made tracks for the Runcorn line, but was knocked into touch. aust before the interval Deakin scored for Runcorn, Richardson improving. Half-time score : • Runcorn, 3 goals 2 trios (12 points), Salford, nil. After the interval Runcorn started without Langley. who was suffering from concussion. Runcorn kept up a strong attack, but the yisitors got to the Runcorn half, when the latter were penalised, Brown placing rt goal. Play then settled in the Salford half, and a foul tackle resulted in the visitors being penalised, Richard- son adding a further goal. Brady then retired hurt, leaving each team with twelve a side. Near the close a forward rush by Runcorn ended in Kennedy scoring at the corner, Richardson failing at goal. Result : Runcorn, 4 goals 3 tries (17 points), Salford 1 goal (2 points).

ST. HELENS v. WIDNES.

Played at St. Helens, before a good attendance. Prescott started for St.Helens,and early on Widnes were Penalised, Traynor experiencing hard lines with his shot at goal. Widnes then opened outa grand passing move- ment, in which all the backs, participated, the result being that Tilley romped over near the posts, for Davies to kick a goal. However, shortly afterwards Whiteley ran over from a splendid forward rush, under the posts, and Toole landed a goal, placing the teams level. Half- time score :—St. Helens 1 goal and 1 try (5 points), Widnes 1 goal and I try (5 points). Widnes restarted the second portion, and were early pressing, the ball going dead. A good kick by Hillen put St. Helens on the aggressive, but the home team being, pen9lised for not playing the ball, Davies kicked a fine goal, giving his side the lead. Whiteley then kicked down, and the home forwards took the ball to the Widnes line, where Traynor got hold and scored at the corner. Toole failed to land a goal. Widnes made several rushes, in which Toole distinguished himself. St. Helens rallied in the closing stages, and Hall picking up dropped a goal. Result :—St.lielens 2 goals and 2 tries (10 points), Widnes 2 goals and I try (7 points).

WARRINGTON JUNIOR LIBERALS. --- Municipal Purity.

PAPERS ON RUSKIN AND CARLYLE.

At their weekly meeting on Monday evening the members of the Warrington Junior Liberal Association were favoured with a visit from three representatives of the Lancashire and Cheshire Federation of British Young Liberals. There was a good attendance and a hearty welcome was extended to the visitors by Mr. Joseph Hawthorn, president of the Association. It was decided that Mr. C. L. Williams, of Stockport,president of the Lancashire and Cheshire Federation, should take the chair. Mr. WILLIAMS said the visitors had heard a great deal of the Warrington Junior Libdrals and they bad not felt satisfied until they had got them to join the Federation. He assured them, on behalf of the Federa- tion, that they would do all in their pea er to make them feel at home and he trusted that the time would come when members of the Warrington Association would hold high offices not only in that Federation but in the National Federation. (Applause.) very good work was being done by the Federation throughout the country. It was most gratify- ing to see such great enthusiasm displayed at the annual conference at Birmingham when Mr. Lloyd George— (applause)—addressed the young bloods. As young bloods they would make mistakes, but they would learn as they went along. He hoped the Warrington young Liberals would look upon the Federation as their own and that their connection with it would be a long and happy one. (Applause.) The young Liberals of Stock- port had taken an active part in the municipal elections. If they in Warrington had not already done something in that direction, he trusted they would in the future. Warrington, no doubt, like Stockport wanted purifying. No doubt they found a difficulty in getting candidates for Council honours to guarantee that they would fight the elections on the purity ticket. They had found a difficulty in Stockport but be was pleased to say that those who had stood on the purity ticket, had fought and won on that ticket. (Applause.) He hoped the Warrington Junior Liberals would do the same at the elections next year. If they could not got the parent Association to guarantee them candidates, he hoped they would bring their own men into the field and back them on the purity ticket. (Applause.) Mr. HAROLD GRAY, One of the , secretaries of the Association, then gave a highly-interesting and inform- ing paper on" Ruskin's social economy." He traced Ruskin's career and pointed out that his early life and I environment were by their exclusiveness not such as '• one would expect to stimulate interest in social I problems. A concern for the well being of the mass of people was soon born in him and, although it must have been a wrench to do so, he ultimately forsook artistic } pursuits for the more serious study of political economy. 1 His first works on the question consisted of essays to ! the" Cornhill Magazine" which aroused such feeling that Thackeray, the editor, declined to accept the last , one. This awoke in Ruskin a resolve that he I would utilize his time to break down the system of I economy which he considered to be false. The great factor which lay at the base of his social criticism was that he dwelt upon social affections instead of 0£ s. d., ! and thought far more of human values than of mere monetary worth. His great object, in fact, was to humanise political econorny. As he himself put it, "the final outcome and consummation of all work was to pro- duce full breath, bright eyes, and happy, hearty human creatures." Although Ruskin's theories commended themselves, the great point was as to how they could be put into practice. Many manufacturers were as desirous as Ruskin to take into consideration these human values, but it was easy to use high flown phrases, whereas when it came to material facts difficulty arose. Ruskin contended that 'manufactuters ought not to produce merely what people wanted, but what 'they actually needed. He also held that the wealth of the nation was not to be judged by its finances but by the possession of great men. Ruskin, who was a Socialist of the right kind, fought nobly for the vast, struggling mass whom no man could number. He used himself as its collective voice and to-day men were rising up in this country who had caught his spirit and would never lay calm until the wrongs which abounded were redressed. (Applause.) Mr. BOYLE, of the Lancashire and Cheshire Federa- tion, having thanked Mr. Gray for the intellectual treat he had afforded them, the deputation left to catch a train. Mr. HAWTHORN took the chair and said Ruskin's views were appropriately summarised in the inscription on the Venetian Church whieh he mentioned in one of his books "Around this temple let the merchant's law be just, his weights true, and his contracts guileless." Ruskin's programme included the establishment of Government training schools in which children should be taught the laws of health, habits of gentleness and justice, and a ditlling by which they could earn their living. His method of dealing with the unemployed was that they should be received at certain centres and compelled to work if they were tit ; and that homes should be provided for the poor and destitute. One of the great ideas mdulding his opinions was that of co-operation. ( A pplause,) An excellent paper on " Carlyle's political teaching " was read by Mr. Burtt. He said that Carlyle insisted on the unity of race and would not tolerate anyone who said " Am I my brother's keeper ?" His earlier Radical- ism was tinged with Conservatism and his later Con- servatism was of a revolutionary type. He, however, maintained an unshaken interest in the welfare of the people and the belief that legislation should have that continually in view. He held that the strongest Government must be the best, that was in which the power was concentrated in the hands of a single individual. He did not think that freedom Was a thing to be desired of itself. The main drift of his political teaching rested in his absolute belief in strength and utter distielief ain what Mazzini called collective wisdom. The march of events, however, was proving the inadequacy of that position. Strong and more practicable points in Carlyle's teaching, some of which had already been attained, mere his insistence on facili- ties for men to rise from the ranks, his advocacy of state-aided emigration, the institution of a minister of education, his dwelling on the duties as well as the rights of land owners, the care of the destitute, the better housing of the poor, and the better regulation of H weights and measures. e always entered a molt vehement protest against the doctrine of laissez-faire. (Applause). An interesting discussion followed, Mr. George Walker pointing out that while they would no doubt agree with Ruskin in his condemnation of cut-throat competition they looked in vain in his works for a remedy. The essayists were cordially thanked on the proposi- tion of Mr. DARBYSIIIRE seconded by Mr. R. LLOYD JONES.

Elias Hurst, a Widnes labourer, who has been con- victed at Warrington, Widnes, Chester, Newcastle, and other places, at Northwich on Thursday was committed for trial on a charge of stealing overcoats,from shop fronts.

The• Examiner, Saturday, November 10, 1906.

===== 0002601_19061110_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REV. JOHN YONGE'S LONG i MINISTRY. • Thirtieth Anniversary Recognized.

Sunday was the thirtieth anniversary of the com- mencement of the Rev. John Yonge's pastorate at Wycliffe Church and the interesting event was fittingly recognized. A pleasant surprise awaited Mr. Yonge,tor on entering his vestry prior to the morning service he found lying on the table an artistically-designed silver rose bowl,filled with choice roses and lilies of the vallay. The gift was accompanied by a card on which was written From the ladies of Wycliffe Church to their dear pastor and his wife, as a small token of their affectionate regard, and with sincere wishes for many happy returns of this anniversary and earnest prayers that both may enjoy health and strength to continue successfully their good work for many years. Wycliffe Church, Warrington." The gift was subsequently laced in the pulpit and during the service Mr. Yonge feelingly acknowledged the kindly spirit which had prompted it. j He said that he was most tenderly touched by the I unlooked-for demonstration' following all that had ' been previously manifested, and added that he was especially grateful for the affectionate way in which Mrs. Yonge was recognized in the presentation. It was, indeed, a pleasant and beautiful surprise, and he accepted it with keen appreciation of the affection it expressed. Mr. Yonge mentioned that it was forty-four years since he commenced his work as a minister in the Isle of Wight. The rev, gentleman preached from the words " Seek the things that are above. Set your affection on things that are above," which. Mr. Yonge ' observed, was a much-needed, but much overlooked, theme of Christian teaching in the present day. At the evening service Mr. YONGE preached to a large congregation from the words "Our Lord Jesus Christ," Romans v., part of verse 1. He said he had that night deemed it well to seek a text that should present upon that anniversary what he had honestly striven, with numberless and serious failures, to make the theme most supremely of that pulpit. Some of them might recall his first sermon as pastor on Sunday I morning, November 5, 1876, and they 'might even remember the text, "From whence eau a man satisfy these men with bread here in the wilderness ?" And the answer to that question, the only possible answer, either then or now, was and is that" Our Lord Jesus Christ " is " the bread of life," the "bread of God which came down from heaven and giveth life unto the world " ; " That is the bread which came down from heaven that a man may eat thereof and not die." He thought he might claim with confidence that the theme of that pulpit during that long period of 30 years, con- sistently and persistently, with loyal faith and earnest purpose, although with many shortcomings, had been Jesus Christ. The preacher 'further accentuated that fact by once more speaking to the congregation of Him, of His alone Savionrhood and Masterhood, His Sufficiency and Sovereignty. They must remember that as often as people had assembled in that Church for worship they had met together in His Name. Whoever constituted the congregation, however many or however few, our Lord Jesus Christ had always been in the midst. If it be true that they, Wycliffe minister and people,and also the neighbours and friends who out of Christian kindness had come with thern,were interested in that anniversary, might they not be con- fident that He also waa interested therein ? Did He not from his perfect knowledge and with His infinite sympathy, -enter fully into the chequered thoughts and tender feelings and pathetic memories which that , thirtieth anniversary produced in them ? He had known I all the persons who had occupied those pews all those years. And He knew them now. They had not passed from His memory even if some of that congregation had forgotten so me of them. The seniors among them would, however, easily recall the names and faces of a very considerable I number who, 30 years ago, occupied those seats and joined in the worship and work of the church. When I they came to think and remember, it was a deeply pathetic circumstance that so large a percentage of the I Wycliffe com uunity in 1876 were now dispersed or passed. Thirty years had made great changes and had i recorded many losses, although, n the providence of God, it had witnessed many accessions and even a goodly net increase in the membership. Of the deacons —Samuel Rigby, Robert Wallace Murray,John Dignum, IJames Stelfox, William Wallington, and Ziba Armitage —only the latter was still with them. Might Mr. and Mrs. Armitage long continue to be with them. Of the members on the church roll when his pastorate began, in number 168, only 28 were now with them. Of the officers in the Sunday school of that time, only two out of 13 remained, and only a similarly small propor- ti on of the teachers of that day were with them now. The seat holders also in the church membership had largely been taken from them either by change of resi- dence or by death. The church, which, in H 76 com- prised' in its avowed church membership less than 170 persons, numbered now some 360 persons, while in the 30 years there had been upwards of 800 received into fellowship. There had been frequent and numerous deaths, and still more numerous removals from the district. Some were worshippers in other churches,and some, unhappily, bad lost their interest in the things of God, but, at least, the figures he had quoted 'testified that the Saviour,had there gathered disciples to Himself even in goodly numbers. But abovetand beyond these must there not be many others whom Christ had " found "there and who had found Christ there, but who might be, as they knew that some were, in many parts of the country and even of the world. Continuing, Mr. Yonge said he was intensely desirous that a great and blessed result should accrue from that anniversary day, and that it would be the renewing of every Chinch ' member's and Church officer's and by no means least, the Pastor's own consecration to our Lord Jesus Christ. They had for many recent months seemed to be on the low levels of Christian profession and service. In face of the material needs of their Church buildings, it might be that they had lost sight somewhat of their spiritual needs and work. By God's goodness they had been anabled to carry through the material work, and were within sight of • its near conpietion. Now let them go on to the spiritual enterprise which belonged to them as a Christian community. They could by cordial combination make that chinch tell upon the town more than ever it had in the past. There was not a church member or attender who could not bring in one new adherent, without robbing any other church, which was a hateful thing to do. He longed to see Wycliffe young men and women enlisting In Christian service and coming into personal Christian service. Would they not all surrender and yield'to His grace? Would they not enlist under His standard, and henceforth live to do His will

True-hearted. whole-hearted, faithful and loyal, King of our lives, by Thy grace we will be, Under Thy standard, exalted and royal, Strong In Thy strength, we will battle for Thee. Peal out the watchword and silence it never, Song of our spirits, rejoicing and free, True-hearted, whole-hearted, now and for ever, King of our lives, by Thy grace we will be. CONGRATULATIONS FROM THE P.S.A. SOCIETY At the close of the Wycliffe P.S.A. meeting, Mr. R. Atkinson moved, and Mr. W. Crosskill seconded, and the Secretary supported, the following motion, which was heartily carried :—" That we heartily congratulate our President on reaching his :10th anniversary as minister of Wycliffe Church ; also his 44th year of his pastoral WOI k, which was commenced in the Isle of Wight. We wish both Mr. and Mrs. Yonge Many happy returns of the day,' and hope for them both many years of health, work, and service." GOOD WISHES FROM WORKINGMEN'S MISSION. Mr. Yonge has received the following letter :- 21, Pickmere-street, Warrington. November sth, 1906. Dear Mr. Yonge,—The following resolution was unani- mously passed at a special meeting of worshippers at the Workingmen's Mission on Sunday at the close of the evening service : "We take this opportunity of expressing to the Rev. John Yonge on the completion of his thirty years' Ministry in Wycliffe Church, our high appreciation of his Christian life and service, and that while his ministry has been chiefly directed and concentrated amongst his own people at Wycliffe Church, it has been repeatedly extended beyond those limitations, and we believe it has inspired and affected alike for good all the Free Churches of the town. We earnestly pray Almighty God that it may please Him to spare in health the life of His faithful servant and that the years to come may if possible be filled with yet more fruitful work for our Glorious Master."—Yours sincerely, ALFRED TILLING, hon. secretary.

1,10;;IES SERVED ON CANAL COMPANY.—The Ship i Canal Company are negotiating with the Manchester and Salford Corporations for the burning of objection- able refuse at their destructors to obviate the nuisance complained of by the local authorities for Ham and Cadishead, Partington aud Bucklow rural district. The last-named body have already decided to serve the Ship Canal Company with an abatement notice, and the TrlarnCouncil instructed their sanitary inspector to do likewise. It is probable that legal proceedings will be obviated.

THAT DAWG SLY,

By S. BARING-GOULD,

Author of "Red Spider," "The Gaverockz," ,"Richard Cable," "The Pennycomequickz," "Cheap Jack Zita," "Mehalah," &c.

Thomas De Vere Winterbottom was a clerk in the office of a chartered accountant, named Sloggett. He had lodged in Norfolk-street, Strand, but was compelled precipitately to change his place of abode. The landlady, Mrs. Fish, had scarlet fever, but kept the matter close till she was peeling, when she , deemed herself sufficiently recovered to attend to ' the cooking and bed-making for her several lodgers. The result was that three of them sickened from scarlet fever. _ _

Thomas De Vere Winterbottom at once resolved on shifting his quarters. The reason for this had got wind, and Mr. Sloggett said to him: "Look her Winter, I'm not going to have you infect the rest of my clerks. We are likely to have a busy time next month, and if my clerks are going to be prostrate and tucked in with scarlatina, I shall be up a tallertree than the ailanthus glandulosus, which is called the Tree of Heaven, because it reaches to the clouds. So you just keep away for a fortnight, till all risk of infection is over; and do not return till you have been put into an oven raised to 212 deg. Fahrenheit, or else have been shut up for forty-eight hours in a room, her- metically sealed, full of sulphur fumes. In such cases, one cannot make too sure. Ta-ta 1" So Thomas De Vere Winterbottom had to shift his quartero, and was given a holiday of a fortnight. He looked about for suitable rooms, and found I hem in a street near Oxford-street.

The landlady said: "Oh, yes! I can take you, but I expect two weeks paid in advance. I have been done by a man named Price, who has gone. He disappeared the night before last, without leav- ing a trace; and, what is worse, he has not taken his dawg with him. I'm not going to pay the tax —seven-and-six--and have to feed the nasty brute as well. He eats as much as a Christian."

"Where is he?" "There, behind you. He's that sort of dawg as always is behind your back." Mr. Winterbottom turned and saw a little mon- grel under the table lookiieg at him with remarkably intelligent eyes. Being fond of animals, the clerk stooped and patted him, and addressed him caress- ingly. The beast winked an eye, and accepted the endearments as his due.

"I think I will retain the dog till his master returns to claim him," said the clerk. "It's my_belief you'll have to keep him a pretty long time then," said Mrs. Giles, the landlady. "And, what is more, I'm not sure as you'll like his tricks. I can't say nothin' for certain, but I misdoubts that there dawg." The beast looked at the clerk and winked again. "What is his name?"

"Well," said the landlady, "his owner used sometimes to call him Sly, and sometimes Slyboots, as the fancy took him." Then said Mr. Winterbottom, coaxingly: "Sly, will you be friends?" The dog, who had seemed perfectly to understand what was going on, responded by putting his wet nose into the clerk's hand, and then, at once, with- out another word, satisfied at the concordat, left the house and vanished up the street. • Thomas De Vera paid his rent for two weeks in advance, and then revisited his former lodgings to remove thence such of his property as was there. On his return, he took a chair and seated him- self by the fireplace, with his feet on the black sheepskin rug, when he noticed something lying there, half-covered by the flocks of wool. He picked it up, and found it vvas a leather purse. On opening it, some silver fell out, and in an inner compartment were three sovereigns in gold. Mr. Winterbottem at once summoned the landlady. "Look her, Mrs. Giles," said he, "your former tenant was not so unjust as you supposed. He left his purse behind him to defray your charges." "I don't think he did that," was the reply. "But," said the tenant, "has anyone else been in the apartment since he left ? " "No, mister, no one." "Then it must be his."

"Well, I don't hold that it is. Howsomdever, as he owes me three pun' six and eightpence ha'penny, 1 don't mind if I do take the money." "That is within a few pence the sum in the purse. You see how wrong it is to judge men prematurely. He was honest, after all. You may as well take the purse likewise, Mrs. Giles." "No, thank you, sir. I'll content myself with ' the money. There is still ilvepence ha'penny.short.” "I'll ut the purse on the mantelshelf till he . returns," said the clerk.

"I don't think he will return," said Mrs. Giles. "And, sir, if I was yOu, I'd get rid of that dawg." "What has,the dog to do with it ? Sly!" Mr. Winterbottom saw the beast under the table watch- ing him with an eye shut, and called to him. Ile caressed him. 'Good dog Brave old Sly! Honest old Slybootsl" Mr. Winterbottom was not a man to care greatly for diversions. He rarely visited a. theatre, and never a music-hall. He spent his evenings in his lodgings cultivating his mind. He may have felt lonely at times, but he had now a dog to keep him company, an entertaining little creature, as he soon discovered, full of tricks ; and the first evening spent in the new apartments passed most agreeably; it was one of the pleasantest in his recollection. For he could talk to and fondle the dog, and make it sit up and beg, pretend to be dead, and jump over his hands. Next day, having nothing particular to engaga him, he resolved on a stroll in one of the parks, and on taking his canine companion with him. Sly trotted .demurely to heel for some time, and then, in the most thronged portion of Oxford- street, Mr: Winterbottom missed him. He was greatly annoyed, for he feared he had lost Sly and his engaging society wholly. However, on his return to his lodgings, he found that the brute had retraced his steps, had waited at the door till it was opened for a lodger, or to take in meat, had dashed within and was lying under the table, with his snout on his paws, waving his tail and with his intelligent eyes full of observation. Thomas Be Vere knelt on the hearthrug, and took out his match-box to put a light to the fire, when, to his astonishment, he found on the sheep- skin a brown-paper parcel, neatly tied up. Without further considering his fire, he took this up, untied it, and found that it contained a gold bracelet, set with brilliants and turquoises, of which latter one had fallen out of its setting, and

armlet was of no very great -value intriniically, du. was handsome, of Indian make. Mr. Winterbottora turned the article about, greatly wondering, and then rang for the landlady. , "Mrs. Giles," said the lodger, "I have found a parcel without address in my room. It contains a gold oriental bracelet set. with jewels. Has anyone been in my room since I left this morning?" "No one that I know of, sir, except the dawg." "But how came thia here? I can hardly have omitted seeing it last night, and the slavey is not likely to have dropned it when cleaning the grate. She does not wear braceets ? " "Hardly, sir." ' "Is this house bewitched ?" "No, sir. There is something written inside the bracelet." "Is there ?"

The clerk took the bracelet to the window. "By Jove! There is. 'Henrietta Fraisne, 16, Cardigan- square. From her dear friend Adela.' There now! I have an address. I can find out about this. You are sure no lady was here who could have dropped this ? "

"Quite sure. And, sir, I think if I was ycn I wouldn't keep that thie dawg." "By Jove ! " exclaimed Mr. Winterpottom. "I'll get at the bottom of this mystery at once. I can but call this afternocn at Cardigan-square. Come along, Sly, old b. y, come along my honest fellow for a little Ivalk."

Sly at once rose and accompanied his new master, trotting at his heels a little way down the street, and then—presto! he was gone. Nor did Mr. Winterbottoin see any more of him till his return home.

Tie took hk way to the square, found .No. 16, and knocked at the door. To the servant who answered he gave his card, and asked to be allowed to speak to Miss Fraisne. The servant ascended the stairs and left Mr. Winterbottom in the hall. Ire returned presently and said that Miss Fmisne was particularly engaged, but that if Itir.Winterbottom would state the purpose of his call she might perhaps manage to give him a minute of her time.

"'Will you be good enough to say that I have found something of value which I suspect the lady has lost." . . .

'The man went again upstairs, and next momer, s charming young lady tripped down. "I beg a thousand pardons. I was holding a skein of silk for my mother, who was winding it off. Will you kindly walk into the breakfast-room?" She led the way. When Mr. Winterbottom was in the apartment with her he brought out the bracelet.

"It is mine," she said. "It was given me by a dear friend, married in India. How did you corric by it? I was taking it to a goldsmith to have tho clasp put to rights—it catches—and a loose turquoise set, and somehow on my way I lost it; how, I cannot for my life, imagine." "And I," replied the clerk, "I, for my life, cannot imagine how it came upon the hearthrug in my Innale lodgings." 4. Then he informed her of his discoveries. He proceeded to tell her of the purse found the pre- vious evening in the same place. "I do not believe in the supernatural, tricksy spirits," said he ; "but, upon my soul, I cannot conceive how else these tricks can have been played on me, unless there be a Puck or Pixie in the house." "Really," said Miss Fraisne, colouring, "you have' do e me an immense favour. I was about to offer a reward for its recovery. Ido not know what to say—how to make an acknowledgment for what you have done, Mr. Winterbottom, but if you would kindly mention—" "There is but one acknowledginent I will hear of," said the clerk, "and that is, to be allowed permission to call again, once only, to tell you how this maddening mystery clears itself up." "You are heartily welcome," she answered. "But do come upstairs and see my mother. She has been so distressed about the loss of the bracelet, and will be vastly interested with your story. Will you oblige me by telling it to her yourself ? " Mr. Winterbottom could noNefuse.

When he left, it was with the old lady's entreaty, united with that of her daughter, that Mr. Winter- bottom would call again when he was able to explain how the bracelet had got into his possession, or, rather, found its way to his hearthrug. The clerk walked home as though treading on air. So charming, so beautiful a girl as Henrietta Fraisne he had never met before. What her circumstances were he had no other means of judging than by estimating the character of house and household. He judged that the family was very'comfortably off, but not wealthy. Oh, that he could solve the mystery The sooner it was solved the better pleased he would be, for then he would rush back to Cardigan-square and see Miss Fraisne once more, and hear her pleasant voice for the last time. • - -

On his way home he looked on all sides for Sly but could not see him. However, on reaching his lodgings the dog was there. Mr. Winterbottom was angry. "Sly," said he, in a tone of reproach, "I am not pleased ivith you. You are idle, sir, idle and lazy. You shirk a walk, and yet exercise is neces- sary for the health. We shall have you suffering from some disorder if you avoid taking constitu- tionals. Halloo! Good gracious I What is that?" I On his hearthrug lay a letter. . I He knelt and picked it up. It WU not in an envelope, consequently had no address. He un- folded it. There was no address at the head of the letter, that ran thus:

"My darling, ever dearest Edward, —I have not seen you for many—too many—days, having been away by the seaside. Oh, bow I ..have pined for you How many pocket-handkerchiefs I have moistened with tears, longing, sighing, aching for your dearest face. And now I insist on our meet- ing at once. The 6th of this month, at half-past five. I shall be at the pastry-cook's at the corner, where we eat cheese-cakes together; after that we will stroll together in the Park. Do not fail me at the pastry-cook's, at the corner of Oxford-street, at half-past five, to-morrow the 6th. My lips burnt for an hour after jou last kissed me. —Ever and eternally yours, " SELINA."

"Good gracious me!" exclaimed Mr. Winter- bottom. 'l'm glad my name is not Edward. But oh, if it had been addressed to Thomas Be Vere and signed Henrietta, it would have been another pack of cards altogether. Bless my soul To-day is the 6th, and in another hour it will be half-past five. lam certain to find the confectioner's shop —at a corner. Come along Slyboots. I am not going to put up with your laziness. Out you shall come, aerate your lungs and circulate your blood. This time you shall not desert." • SO saying, Thomas De Vere took a piece of twine and attached it to the collar of the dog. Then he went forth, Sly attending him, but much out of humour at being deprived of his liberty, and inclined to drop sulkily into the rear. The clerk looked about him as he walked along Oxford-street. It is a long thoroughfare, and no indications were given in the letter as to the side on which the place of rendezvous was. "Oh, those mad, passionate lovers!" thought he. , "How youth and ardent blood surmount all prudential barriers. They are certain to be punc- tual. I should be so if I had received such a letter from Henrietta." At that moment he heard a clock strike the half-hour, and simultaneously two persons, the one a gentleman and the other a lady, entered a pastrycook's at a street corner opening into Oxford-street. ,

I Mr. Winterbottom followed, full of confidence, and, passing within, heard the gentleman address his companion with the query: "What will you have, my dear?" "Cheese-cakes," replied the lady.: I Instantly the clerk laid his hand on the sleeve of the gentleman and asked: "Is your Christian name Edward?" ; The person addressed turned round. He was a heavy, stout man, well advanced in years, with drooping cheeks, and dark whiskers turning grey. ; There was the commercial look about him strong. He was either a stockjobber or a shipbroker. The lady was middle-aged, by no means elegant in shape or beautiful in feature. Her complexion was choleric and her eye volcanic. ; "My name is Edward," answered the gentleman. , "But pray what is that to you?" I "It is now half-past five," said Winterbottoin, solemnly. "If you desire to know the precise time," said the stranger, looking at his watch, "it is 5.38." j "And she asked for cheese-cakes," said Winter bottom, gravely and impressively. "She—sir ! Whatedo you mean by she?" "She—why, Selina." "Edward, is this man mad?" asked the lady. "How dare's he call me Selina when my name is: Maria Antoinette." "I beg your pardon," said. Mr. Winterbottom. "There has been some extraordinary mistake. And yet, this is a corner shop. I must justify myself." He dropped the string by which he held the dog and. produced the letter from his pocket. "Look, sir Look, madam ! Here you have it all. Edward —half-past. five o'clock—cheese-cakes—a confec- -4.4----- ecirmar " .

"Edward I' exclainied file iauy, =axing up. "This does, indeed, demand an explanation. Au. I your true and long-suffering wife, and you—you go making appointments with young ladies—ladies, indeed !—at pastry-cooks'." "I beg pardon," explained the clerk. "It is the lady who makes the appointment." "The point is immaterial. Oh, you Don Juan! You Lothario Sighing and aching for the sight of your flabby face, indeed !" "I know nothing about this. I never met any Selina in, my life, protested the frightened man, breaking into globules of perspiration. "A corner shop. Oh, how cunninf. Moistening a thousand pocket-handkerchiefs witii sentimental tears. Gah

"There are other corner shops," said the man, in a trembling voice. "I vow and protest—" "I also vow and protest," interrupted the angry "wife. "Lips burning for a week after your last kiss, indeed! I suppose you hadn't shaved before you administered it." "This is insupportable," gasped the commercial man. Ahd, turning on Mr. Winterbottom, he demanded, in an agitated voice: "What is the meaning of this? eknow not a word about this. It is an insult, an outrage. Why have you done this? What is your name ? Where do you live? Who the deuce are you? Why is this abominable trick played on me?" "Here is my card," said Mr. Winterbottom, "I am extremely embarrassed and distressed that I Should have fallen into so ghastly a mistake." "Oh, this is no mistake. He is capable of every- thing, stout and elderly though he be," exclaimed the lady. Alarmed lest he should be further involved in the conjugal tempest he had inadvertently roused, Mr. Winterbottom fled the confectioner's, and. hastened home.

But the dog had preceded him, and when he entered his room was lying under the table, licking his lips and winking. #

Onsthe hearthruereposed a silver card-case! "Here's a go!" exclaimed the clerk, and stood and contemplated this last deposit in consterna- firm. He at once lighted the gas. and the silver j case glittered as it reflected the et.. "Was there ever such a diablerie as this enacted before? be hanged if I touch it! I shall go off my head." He retreated to his bedroom, where he washed his sands. On his return, the case was still on the rug. tie stooped, picked it up, and placed it on his table. At the same rament the door opened, and the gentleman, whom he took to be a stock- broker, entered, heated and angry. "I have come to know the meaning of this, Mr. Winter—Winter "—he referred to the card, and concluded "bottom." Then, after stertorous breathing, he resumed: "You have caused un- pleasantness—to use a mild expression—between me and my wife. lam as innocent as a babe unborn. I never knew a Selina. Be blowed to them! Selinas, indeed! Hallool What have you here? My wife's card-case! A silver family heirloom. What is the meaning of this ? He opened it, and drew out—not a card, but a photograph of his wife. "Hah " he shouted, and stamped about the room. "I see it all, in its hideous wickedness. She has been here. She—involved in a miserable intrigue with you. She has given to you her carte de visite and this silver family heirloom. Oh, nothing is' too good for her inamorata and that affair of the letter was a basely contrived plot, a diabolical plot, devised against me, me innocent as the saints in light, so as to throw me off my guard. Hah hah hah !" He twisted about the room stamping. "I will dash this photograph into her face; I will hurl at her my denunciation of a vile intrigue with a Winter—Winter "—he-referred again to the card, and concluded with—"bottom." "Sir," said the clerk, with dignity, "you are most unreasonable. I absolutely deny the charge.* "How then came this card-case and photograph in your possession? I pause for a reply." Mr. Winterbottom was silent. He could not answer the question. At this juncture steps were heard on the stairs, the door was opened, and a couple of policemen entergd, followed by Mrs. Giles. "There's the dawg," said the landlady, "under the table, with his tail between his legs, and both eyes shut and snoring. I knew he was a bad 'un, and up to mischief."

"And Price lodged here P " asked the chief of tl,e two constables.

_ "Yes," answered Mrs. Giles, "till two nights agone, when he decamped." "And who may these gentlemen be ? " Mr. Winterbottom gave his name. "I have but just engaged the rooms." he said. "Sir, said the policeman, "a notorious thief called—it is one of his aliases—Price, has been arrested. He has, been in the habit of emploxing a dog—that dog there, sleeping so sweetly--:Eno, hang me! he has one eye open—employing that dog to pick pockets for him. Have you noticed any signs of roguery 4,et the brute?" "Four," Mr. Winterbottom replied. "The last —this card-case, stolen in a pastrycook's shop, whilst the lady was addressing her husband." Then the clerk told his tale.

The stockbroker grew cool and collected, And wiped himself down with his silk pocket-handker- chief. "My good fellows," said he to the con- stables, "if you will step round to my house with the dog and tell the story to my wife—she wouldn't believe me if I were to tell it, and were not corro- borated by you—l will give you each a sovereign and 'a couple of glasses amy best port. And you, sir "—to Mr. Winterbottom—"will you do me the favour of dining with inc " "I should be most happy," the clerk replied. "But that I am duo at No 16, Cardigan-square." "Well—dine with me to-morrow.

Mr. Winterbottom sped to the house in the square, sent up his card, and, notwithstanding the hour, was readily admitted. He was now able to give the entire story and solve the mystery. He was invited to stay for dinner by Colonel Fraisne, a merry old Indian officer.

That was but the first of several visits, and hie dinner next day was also the first of several with the ship-broker and his wife, who both took a fancy to him. The old gentleman presently offered Thomas De Vere a situation in his office, at much better pay than he had received at Sloc,'ett's, and without exacting of him that he should be doubly disinfected. It is not improbable that eventually he may become a partner. . - . .

It is also not improbable that his visits to No. 16, Cardigan-square may also lead to a partner- ship, but of another kind. And all this was brought about by That Dawg Sly. [Lir END.

In a book just published IN THE CHINESE containing short character COMPOUNDS. sketches of members of the

House cf Lords, it is remarked of the ARCHBISHOP OF CANTERBURY that "He has also felt it his duty to show that he is not led away by the outcry against Chinese slavery, but then," it is added, "Dr. DAVIDSON is a great believer in facts." The author of these words chose an unfortunate moment for despatch- ing them to the printer. The facts have come upon us and the ARCHBISHOP OF CANTERBURY in a flood, so much of a flood, indeed, that they have had to be dammed up. The main body of the evidence presented at the inquiry held by Mr. CONSUL BUCKNILE is not to he published, it is being privately whispered among members of Parliament, the sad truth being that it will not bear publication, that it is revolting, ghastly, unspeakable. Yet it is evidence at which few persons in their senses can honestly affect to be surprised. Just that has occurred which was absolutely certain to occur. The circumstances of life in the Chinese compounds are not such as can ,be hinted at. They were clearly foreseen, never- theless. The late Government was warned, South Africa was warned, the country was warned. It was only those who deliberately closed their eyes to facts, who gave their consent to this dreadful, soul-debasing experiment with Chinese labour in the South African mines.

The ARCHBISHOP OF.CANTERBURY had a magnifi- cent opportunity. His great position gave him the power to stay the hand of the late Government at the deciding moment. The Chinese Ordinance would not have been signed if Dr. DAVIDSON, instead of describing the importation of the Chinese as "a regrettable necessity," had said what he must surely have felt—that the intended experiment was opposed to civilisation, religion, and morality. We will not, however, speak further of the ARCHBISHOP OF CANTERBURY'S share in the dreadful business. His own mind will now, no doubt, be full enough of reproaches. Indeed we envy none of those who, in a passion to repair the damages of tile 'South African War, forgot the history and the traditions of our country with regard to free- dom; forgot too our mission in the fore- front of civilised nations,consented to all that was asked for, and defended all that was done by the unspeakable speculators of the Rand. It is to be said for the ARCHBISHOP Of CANTERBURY that be made certain conditions which he understood were to be kept. They were not kept. None of the conditions made by the Randlords have ever been kept. They imported into South Africa vast hordes of celibate Chinamen. Contrary to promise, they recruited many of them tfroin the prisons. Again, contrary to promise, they slipped through their liability to pay £30,000,000 as part of the cost of the war, and they have evaded the con- dition that Chinamen should be employed only on unskilled labour.

In an answer made ,in the House of Commons the other day to an indiscreet question put by Sir GILBERT PARKER, the Under-Secretary for the Colonies showed that whilst the Chinese employed in the mines had increased in number by 3,598 during six months, the whites employed had decreased by 1,000 during the same period. What MT. LYTTLETON assured the House of Commons was that the employment of Chinese would lead to a much greater employment of whites. He seems to have said anything that the Randlords told him to say. He said over and over again that the Chinese would not be employed on skilled labour, yet that they are being so employed is proved not only by direct statements to that effect, but by the fact that the whites, wtho were all skilled labourers, are being discharged from the mines. Meantime it comes out that Lord SELBORNE, the High Commissioner, has been most indiscreetly interfering in politics on behalf of Sir PERCY FITZPATRICK, whose book, "The Transvaal from Within," was one of the main , incitements to the war, and who is one of the j most infatuated of the defenders of the Chinese "experiment." There can be no doubt that Lord SELBORNE ought to be brought home with the least possible delay. His policy is not that of the Government or of the English people, and after the revelations oow made the people will not put up with further nonsense, the purpose of which is to make permanent the present infamous condition of things on the Rand.

Our congratulations are THE MAYORALTY. cordially extended to Mr. . .

Alderman Smethurst, the new Mayor of Warrington. Alderman Pierpoint having declined the honour, the nest man with a strong title to the position was certainly Alder- man Smethurst. Probably no member of the Council has been so painstaking in his attendance to public duties as the genial chairman of the Elec- tricity and Tramways Committee, and in his new and wider sphere of activity he can be relied upon to show the same diligence in tha public interest Alderman Smethurst has donned the Mayoral habiliments at a time when, perhaps, a stronger man would have been desirable, but we believe he will endeavour to make up for any deficiencies of temperament by a firm resolve to show absolute impartiality. He only needs the cordial and con- siderate support of the members of the Council to make his year of office a source of personal pleasure and public util;ty. That support, we hope, will always be forthcoming.

In another column we pub- Mn. C. E. PARKER lish a long letter from Mr. AND THE C. E. Parker, the President ELECTIONS, of the Liberal Association, _ . . . .

in the course of which he takes a survey of the municipal elections, and particularly the one in L Atchford Ward. Mr. Parker is showing his interest in the elections rather late in the day, and we cannot congratu- late him on the view he takes of the issue in Latchford. We should have thought that a man of Mr. Parker's attainments and standing in the town would have risen above the rather elementary idea that because a Councillor aceepts the duties of Mayor's Auditor he should be paid for his services. The whole principle, of the Non- Trading agitation is that no man shall profit by or receive payment for any services rendered in his Icapacity as a member of the Corporation. Mr. , Parker expresses a desire "to help to restore muni- cipal peace, allay hitter feelings, and heal the dissensions which have been made lay this unhappy controversy." Then he candidly endorses "the view of the Conservative Chairman in expressing. opposition to immoral trading," hut adds that there is a diversity of opinion in defining what immoral trading is. We regret that we must regard Mr. Parker as being both illogical and ieconsidtent. The best way to "restore muni- cipal peace" is not to attempt to upset the generally accepted Non-Trading prin• ciple. This principle is seriously challenged by the conduct of a Mayor's auditor who enters the Council to serve the ratepayers, and accepts £3O of public money for public service under a provision the legality of which is widely 'questioned but the impropriety of which is undoubted. Yet our correspondent upholds the Mayor's auditor in his acceptance of a payment from the rates. Perhaps we can convince Mr. Parker of the questionableness of the code of public morals he supports by an illustration. Suppose the fee for the Mayor's auditor was £3OO instead of £3O. Would it not then be perfectly reasonable to suggest that the man who accepted the office had an ulterior motive in entering the Council ? Perhaps we should not impute motives, but the fact remains that there would be considerable competition for the post if it had an emolument attached to it which repre- sented a comfortable income. No man of honour would allow himself to be placed in such a position. Whether £3O or £3OO the morality of the question, and its gravity from the point of view of the muM- cipal purist, remain the same. Mr. Parker knows as well as anyone that "no man can serve two masters" and how he can conceive that a Town Councillor with any pretensions to consistency may faithfullyeserve the ratepayers with one hand and pocket £3O of their monies with the other, passes our comprehension. The moral aspect of the situation is, in our view, clear as daylight and incapable of two interpretations. In another part of his letter Mr. Parker makes some animadversions on "the local Liberal ' paper " and at the same time seriously misinter- prets its views. We never suggested,as Mr. Parker says we did, that Liberals should "follow their leanings in Imperial politics in an election in which a candidate, who happens to be a Liberal, expressly states that he is not fighting on political grounds, but for a policy with which they do not agree." Our criticisms, last week, were plainly directed against the Liberal Ward Committee for doing what, as a party organisation, it had no right to, viz., to pass a resolution in favour of the official Tory nominee. With the members of this Committee it was not a question of politics, the payment of an elective auditor, or anything so legitimate, but one of personality pure and simple. They were determined to prevent Mr. Dodds entering the Council, and they sacrificed their principles to secure an unworthy end. This is the conduct about which we expressed our "abhorrence." Mr. Parker is perhaps not acquainted with the wheels within wheels in this election. Until he is, he should refrain from pass- ing any hasty judgment. We would rather see the electors voting for the best man in municipal elections, independently of party political con- siderations. Mr. Parker says he would have voted for Mr. Wilson. Would he have been supporting the best man, or would he have been setting back the cause of municipal purity for which he pro- fesses some concern ? This is a question we suggest for Mr. Parker's rcaturer consideration.

The Town Council was WORK DONE supposed to have completed AND UNDONE. at Tuesday's caucus -meet- ing all the routine work requiring confirmation yesterday, but a good deal of it required doing over again arid in a differsnt way altogether. Some members confessed to having changed their views in the interval between Tuesday and Friday, while many others had apparently done the same thing, but did not like acknowledging it. The Standing Orders were carefully revised at the caucus meeting and several important changes agreed upon. Yesterday they were re-revised and in some cases altered back again. Standing Order 30, as printed, stipulated that "not more than three Aldermen shall be elected on any Committee." The Alder- men were, naturally, indignant and succeeded in ; getting the order withdrawn. Whatever the ' tendency of the times may be, Aldermen are still Aldermen, and until they are abolished by statute they are entitled to be considered as men of experience and sagacity beyond that of the common Councillor. To endeavour to limit their usefulness was an unkind slap at their office, which we do not think the ratepayers would approve of. In the appointment .of C3mmittees the harshly-treated Aldermen were, in a largo measure, restored to office--which was the only just and politic course open to the Council.

There is one new stand- MURDERING THE ing order (No. 10) which is MINUTES. of quite a revolutionary

character, and yet little attention was drawn to it. This order refors to the vexed question of the minutes of Committees of the Council, which has been settled and unsettled time and agaio. Not long ago it was decided—unwisely we think—to print citl the minutes on the agenda paper and send them to the press. If there was anything which it would not do to puhlish, the Chairman of each Com- mittee was supposed to indicate the particular item with a" star "end leave all to the discretion of the newspapers. There has only been one trial of this method, and the consequence was the appearance on the minutes of items much more insignificant than the "parish pump." Now, by the new standing order, all the minutes are to be printed, hut kept rigorously from the press, and of course, the public, We should say that the latest edict will last about one month before being replaced by some provision of a saner nature. Toe press has only to retaliite by declining to report the meet jogs of the Council for the latter to see the error of its tortuous ways with remarkably clear vision. Standing order 10 means secrecy with a vengeancEN and the experience of the past few years does not lead us to think that the public will tolerate such a condition of things for more than the period necessary in which to get the or der repealed.

In a current issue the TETE "SPEAKER " AND " Speaker " rather roughly NATIONAL SERVICE. handles the National Ser-

vice question in response to a letter from a well-known Warrington Leaguer. The famous weekly says editorially: " We have never been inclined to agree with . those Liberals who pooh-pooh tbe conscriptionist agitation. It is a thing, in our opinion, which ought to be strenuously combated. Lord Roberts and his friends have captured the headmasters of the public schools, numbers of University dons, and plenty of influential people in the War Office. With the I exception of the "Tribune," all the penny morn- ing papers of London are advocates of compulsory service, and on Thursday the " Tunes," whicth three or four years ago was arguing for reducing the army estimates to about twenty millions, of course without conscription, finds itself thrown back upon the view so ably advocated by Lord R )herts, that if ws want real security at a reduced exper.diture we must make national defence the legal duty of every able bodied citizen. We print in our correspondence columns a letter from a Warrington manufacturer who worships at the German shrine, and doubtless looks forward to the day when the Mayor of War- rington will reproduce the glorious example of the Burgomaster of Kopenick. But,seriously is it not time that this House of Commons should pass a very strong resolution condemning Lord Roberts's. propaganda? A day spent in carrying such, a motion by a majority of, say, ton to one, would certainly not be wasted, and it would have the additional advantage of forcing Mr. Balfour to give the country another proof of his political embarrassment."

The modern taste for a THE ARISTOCRATIC smattering of education and "OUT-op-WORK," a black coat does not lead" to much prosperity. It is easy to be nothing in particular when the profes- sion is not overcrowded. But nowadays the tendency is for a certain class of parents to bring up their sons with tine ideas, genteel airs, a smattering of the dead languages, a nodding acquainance with the living, and an uncertain knowledge of shorthand—the last as a concession to the vulgar demand for at least one useful accomplishment. When these young gentlemen leave school they look around with a vague idea of "getting something." They think they will write for the papets, but soon find that the papers do not require them. Then they turn to the chance of getting a secretaryship, life with an independent gentleman, who prohably owns a motor-car and a yacht, and allows the secretary to use his box at the Opera, having decided attractions. One such independent gentle- man advertised in a London paper the other day for a secretary. Two days after he put in another advertisement, asking that no further applications for the situation should be sent, because the letters in reply to the first advertisement had been so numerous that they could not all be opened! Here is a social problem: What shall we do with our genteel unemployed? How much better it would be if the elucatiorr upon which the parents of these young men had spent money had been dieected to giving them a definite and useful career. The thing of the future for evety young man, rich or poor, is a trade or profession. PaWio schools are largely to blame for encouraging the neglect of professional and technical training.

Two very amusing errors are to be found on the public notice in front of the Town Hall declaring the results of the November elections, and both errors are made by Tory Aldermen whose names have been mentioned in connection with the Mayoralty. They must have been suffering from electoral fever on the night of November Ist., or surely one would never have written " one sploit paper," and the other "two spoilt pappers." * *

How near cleanliness is to Godliness is not always realised. According to Mr. A. H. Crosfiell M.P., soap and civilization go hand in I,,hand, until the con- sumption of soap almost amount ls to an index of the progress of civilization. Missionaries ought to be armed with a cake of soap—Perfection of course— as well as the Bible when endeavouring to dissipate the abysmal ignorance of the heathen. By removing dirt they would doubtless get nearer the spirit. * * *

The Rev. John Yonge's thirtieth anniversary at Wycliffe Congregational Church was quietly but fittingly celebrated on Sunday. Mr. Yonge conducted the services at his church and there were very large congregations which included many well-wishers from other churches in the town.Durina the day Mr.and Mrs. Y onge were the recipients of a handsome silver rose- bowl from the ladies of the church. We join them in wishino• Mr. and Mrs. Yoncre continued health and happiness in their association with Wycliffe Church.

* * The Rev. Arthur Mursell, who lectured at Warring- ton on Tuesday evening, is an exceptionally rapid speaker, and often astounds his audiences by his extraordinary flow of words. He never fails to appre- ciate the humorous side of things, and has a happy way of relating the varied experiences which have fallen to his lot at home and abroad. He is a veteran minister but retains surprising activity, and the lecture which he delivered on Tuesday was not the least enjoyable of several which Warrington people have had the privilege of hearing from him. o

The tree-planting question at Stockton Heath seems to have simmered down and the only thing to excite the minds of the tree-planters who met on Tuesday evening was a letter which had been sent to the Rural Council by Mr. Hough, one of the most uncom- promising opponents of the scheme. Mr. Hough wrote to remind the authority that the foggy weather was coming on and urged on behalf of the "aged, infirm, and near sighted " the removal from narrow footpaths of " those mementoes of insanity, mostly in the shape of a dead stick enclosed in a massive iron case." This is rather unkind of Mr. Hough. Did not the "dead sticks " put forth enough leaves last summer to choke the gullies ? At least the County Council says 30 ! * * *

Were I a man (says " Rita " in the " Daily Chronicle ") I would never grant the suffrage tot,„,' women until they had taken "first honours "in ths!ll following classes : Sensible dressing for the street. Economical housekeeping. Sense of motherly responsibility. Absolute temperance. No cigarette smoking. No gambling or betting. Rational scheme of education for girls einbmen-, ing usefulness, and the discarding of senseless accomplishments. Evenness of temper and the power of logical argu- ment. No hysterical fads and crazes. No senseless imitation of manly sports.

Warrington people will be pleased to hear that an honorary canorrry of the Cathedral church of St. Peter, Liverpool, has been conferred upon ,the 'Rev. Henry Gibson Smith, M.A., Vicar of Allerton. Before ordination Mr. Smith was second master of Warrington 130teler Grammar School. His first curacy was at St. Paul's, Warrington, 1881, whence he was transferred to Childwall, 1885, to be promoted in 1891 rector of Halewood. In 1902 he was installed vicar of Allerton.

'The Late Mr. P. Bate. On Monday at the Warrington Cemetery were interred the remains of Mr. Peter Bate, late of 23, *Rolleston•street, one of Warrington's oldest and most •highly esteemed Catholics. The deceased gentleman, who was in his 73rd year, passed away on Thursday morning, the Feast of All Saints. The greater portion of his life was spent as a member of St. Alban 's Church, Bewsey-street, for which mission, from almost its earliest 'days, he was one of the most enthusiastic workers. His labours were not, however, i-, confined to his own parish,but are well remembered in connection %vial the Churches of St. Mary's, Latch- ford, St. Mary's, Battermarket-street, and the Passionist r'Monastery at Sutton, near St Helens. A solemn 'Requiem Mass for the repose of his soul was sung at St. Alban's on Monday morning, the celebrant being ,the Rev. Father Whittle, '0.5.8. The music .(Gregorian) was admirably rendered by a' ' choir, lead by the Revs. Father Buggins and Ryiance, 0.5.13., assisted at thel organ by ?Mr. J. B. Caldwell. Amongst the mourners 4 —were Messrs. John, Joseph, Peter, William, Aloysius, -and Benedict (sons of the deceased), Masters Peter, , Bernard, and James (grandsons), Mr. Thomas Thamp- -son (Patricroft, cousin), the Rev. Fathers Roche and ' Marrs (St. Mary's, Latchford), Brother Henry (Sutton. Monastery), Mr. George Johnson (representing Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons, Ltd., for whom the 'deceased worked f r nearly 43 years)„, Mesdames Joseph, Peter, William and Aloysius Ba'be (daughters-in-law), Miss Mary Bate (granddaughter\ Mrs. Walter Thompson (Leyenshulnae), Messrs. John Hesketh, Robert Sutton, Joseph Poilinore and W. Scotson. The bearers were Messrs. Dilworth, Delaney, Eckersley, Lawton Owen Toole and Austin Vernon. A large number of beautiful wreaths were sent by members of the family and others of the deceased's many friends.
