TABLE, FLOOR, AND HANGING L Amrs in large variety. Latest patterns.—J. S. GARLAND, Iron- monger, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. Warrington Draughts League. An exhibition of simultaneous draughts-playing was given by Mr. Frank Dunne, the Warrington "International " on Thursday evening, in the Golborne-street (Baptists) Clubroom, in aid of the Warrington League funds. A team of twenty, includ- ing with but few exceptions all the strongest players of the League, was opposed to the expert, and some fine play was shown, Mr. Dunne remark- ing that there was a vast difference in the calibre of his present opponents, and those he used to "walk round" some ten or twelve years ago. The contest occupied a little over two hours, the score at the conclusion being : —Dunne won, 18 ; lost, 1 ; drawn, 4 ; total 23 games. The win was scored by Mr. W. Tomkinson, and the draws by F. Kenny, J. Rowe, J. Hamblett and E. J. Powell. .

Wool) CHIMNEY PIECES, Fire Grates and Tam unrivalled selection, inspection invited.—J. S, GARLAND, Builders' Ironmonger, &c., 51 to 55, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. Alterations to Licensed Premises. _

At the Warrington Police Court on Monday Mr. Browne applied for permission to make certain alterations at several licenced houses. At the Patten Arms Hotel it was proposed to build a larger and better equipped lavatory, Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Co., who only came into possession on October 26th, being desirous of putting the place in, a thoroughly sanitary state at once. They also proposed to make further sanitary arrangements in the yard. In regard to the Glass Maker's Arms, it was proposed to make the sanitary and domestic arrangements complete. They wished to erect a new scullery, to improve the yard, &c., and to make matters safer for persons serving behind the counter. The Chairman : Is there any sign of the houses being wanted for street improve- ment? Mr. Browne did not think so, remarking that they intended to make a straight line. With reference to the King's Arms Inn, Mr. Browne applied for the committee's sanction to certain sanitary and domestic alterations, and added that the drinking area would not be increased in any way. The coin- mitten retired to consider the application, and on their return, the Chairman said the three plans were accepted, on the undertaking being given that no future application would be made to increase the present drinking area, and that the altertions now proposed were only to meet domestic and sanitary purposes. Mr. Browne gave the required undertaking. " HERALD " KETCTIF,N RANGES and " Peveril " Fire Grates are hard to best.—Sole agent, J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge it., Warrington.—Advt. Police Court and Prison Gate Mission. • •

The following are the principal items in the work of the Police Court and Prison Gate Mission of the Liverpool Diocesan Church of England Temperance Society for October. The Mission Room near Walton Prison has been open every morning at the time of the discharge of prisoners, and an aggregate of 714 persons have availed themselves of the missionaries' offer of breakfast, advice and other assistance. At the police courts 247 visits have been made, and 1387 persons have been spoken to in the courts and cells, of whom 515 have been subsequently visited in their abodes. 51 women, girls and boys have been takeic to homes, sent back to friends, or provided with work. 33 tickets for work have been given for the Society's Firewood and Mat Factory, Bootle. Articles of clothing have been provided in 42 instances to enable the recipients to obtain work. 228 letters have been I written in the efforts to rescue and reclaim persons. j 282 men, women and young people have come from the police courts and prisons to the Head Offices to be assisted in various ways.. The magistrates have had the help of the missionaries in 110 instances, and the court officials and the police in 98 instances, 206 pledges have been administered, and 54 meetings ddressed.

Shorthand Writers' Association. The large attendances at the meeetings of this Association which prevailed at the commencement of the season's work are still being maintained, the gathering at the Winmarleigh Cafe on Wednesday night being no exception. After speed practice, which is an important feature of the Association's work, the Hon. Secretary (Mr. Joseph Marsh) gave a short, but instructive lecture on a phonographic principle. The latter portion of the evening was occupied by the reading by Mr. H. A. Webb of an entertaining paper entitled " My Career as influenced by Shorthand." The speaker narrated in a most interesting and racy style the experiences grave and gay—of his somewhat eventful life; his remarks being listened to witll great attention. The gist of Mr. Webb's autobiograp y proved to be a very strong argument against the al ost threadbare ques- tion as to whether or not a knowledge of shorthand hinders a clerk from getting on in mercantile life. In his case such a knowledge had been of the greatest assistance in many ways, but especially so in regard to the obtaining by him of better appointmfmts in the commercial world. To him Pitman's Phonography had been a veritable "stepping stone to higher things." Several of the members favourably commented upon the paper, and the meeting terminated with the customary vote of thanks. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASIIIRE.—Advt. Shunting Fatality at Warrington. 1 Mr. Brighouse held an inquest at Warrington on Tuesday, touching the death of Samuel Rowland Barrow (41), a porter employed by the London and North-Western Railway Company, and who resided at 7, Sandhill-terrace, Wash-lane, Latchford. Mr. Steel watched the interests of Joseph Pennington, engine driver, and William Fairclough, the signal- man ; while Mr. Browne represented the widow. The , Coroner stated that on Thursday at about one o'clock Barrow was engaged in shunting operations at Bank Quay Station. He was instructed to detach a brake van from some coaches. He got between the van and the last coach for the purpose of unscrewing the couplings, but finding he could not do it be came out and gave the driver the signal to "ease up." Barrow again went between the van and the coach to unscrew the couplings, and while dilfig this the coach struck him in the back, causing him to fall on the rail and three of the coaches passed over him, injuring his arm badly.—Dr. Ilodgkinson, senior house surgeon at the Infirmary, said Barrow was suffering from fatty disease of the heart. Death was due to shock caused by the laceration of the arm muscles and contusion of the chest. Joseph Pennington, the engine driver in question, detailed the facts connected with the shunting opera- tions, and said Barrow gave him the signal to set back." As soon as witness started the train he missed Barrow and immediately stopped. He got down from his engine and remained by the deceased until the ambulance arrived. A verdict of "Accidental death " was returned, and the driver was exonerated from all blame.

The mother-in-law has !ong been a butt for public • jocularity and sarcasm, but at last we have heard of one who "got even " with her tormentor. In a news- paper published in Neustadt, in Sasony, an advertise- ment recently ran : "FO7 disposal.—A mother-in-law ! She has an infernal temper and a wart on her nose, but has considerable means. Shall be happy to pave the way for anyone who will come forward with a real view to marriage." In a couple of days appeared the reply "I am much obliged for the attempt to get me re-married, but unless I can get a husband who, unlike my son-in-law, is not a pitiful lying impostor, who would sell his soul for drink, I prefer to remain as I am." •

When is a dog not a dog? was the question raised during the hearing of a summons at Lambeth last week. Mr. F. W. Tonkin, in defending a man sum- mondecl for allowing a dog to be at large unmuzzled submitted that in the eves of the law a dog is not a dog until it is six months old. "Then what is it ?" queried the magistrate. Mr. Tonkin : It is an embryotic specimen of the canine race. Mr. Hopkins.: " Embryotic specimen of the canine race" sounds rather like a dog. Hr. Tonkin : It is no mora a dog than a egg is a bird or spawn roe a fish. In order that proof might be given as to the (late of the dog's birth, Mr. Hopkins adjourned the hearing.

He was touched by the refer- ence to his previous mayoralty, and with the kindly references to Mrs. Bishop. He would, under all the circumstances, accept the office, and hoped to have the support of the whole of the members of the Council (Applause.) At the Council meeting at noon on Friday Alderman Bishop was formally elected to the office.

TREE PLANTING AT STOCKTON HEATH.

Action of the District Council.

The inhabitants of Stockton Heath are getting some- what weary of the constant bickering over the question of the planting of trees in the district, with the result that there was only a small attendance at the adjourned annual meeting of the Tree Planting Committee on Tuesday. Mr. Plinston presided. The Chairman pointed out that the Stockton Heath Parish Council had asked the permission of the Rural Council to appoint three gentlemen to meet the three appointed by the Council so that they could consult together about tree planting. The Garden Society had had no written communication from the District Council, but he (the Chairman) had seen Mr. Pilling, who told him that there would be no opposition. Mr. Pilling also added that he did not think there would be any necessity to take up any trees whatever. (Applause) Mr. H. Hallard : The result is that the opposition is practically withdrawn ' • but there are mutual com- mittees appointed for the future and not for the past. , The Chairman : Yes, for the future. Mr.O. Brown called tbe attention of the meeting to a letter from Mr. William Hough of ‘Vhitefield-road, which was read at a meeting of the Runcorn Rural, Council last week. Mr. Hough wrote :—"May I respect-i fully remind you that we are now approaching tlie dark and foggy days of winter. On behalf of the hundreds of memorialists and more especially aged, infirm and near-sighted, that they may receive at your hands that protection necessary for their safety by removing off narrow footpaths those mementoes of insanity, tnostly in the shape of a dead stick, enclosed in a massive iron case, thus creating a glaring and dangerous nuisance to all and any of -his Majesty's loyal subjects who wish to walk along in safety without fear of accidents. There is no necessity to use any iron hand.' All that is required is a Christian feeling towards the above-named, tempered with a little discretion and common sense. I venture to say that the funds for dumping down upon the residents these unwelcome nuisances are largely from non-residents of Stockton Heath. You, gentle- men, are the the responsible party for any accidents that may occur, and it is unnecessary for me to ask that you will endeavour to protect yourselves and also the above-named, and, yours faithfully, WILLIAM Hoton.." Mr. Brown said he hoped the meeting would express a very strong disapprobation of what Mr Hough had written. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman observed that there were not more than half a dozen subscribers who were not directly ; interested in the district. After some discussion it was decided to take no further notice of the letter. The Chairman : We shall have to obtain permission to plant trees, but I don't think there will be any dull- culty now we have the six gentlemen to deal with. If the trees are taken up now they will have to be taken ,up by the District Council. The Parish Council will refuse to take them up, and it will throw the onus on the District Council.

CHURCH CLUBS BILLIARD LEAGUE.

Trinity in their inital match in the 2nd Division of the above League met St. Pauls at Bewsey-road, and had to retire defeated by 25 points. The meeting of these two old rivals created a great deal of interest and not until the winning stroke was made did the enthusiasm of players and spectators abate any. The Trinitarians were champions of the 2nd Diviston last ! seasdn and St. Pauls were naturally elated with their 1 victory, this being the second win for them in the 1 tournament. Below are narticulars of the scores : ST. PAUL'S 2ND. TRINITY 2ND. H. Pennington E. H. Mann 2q J. J. Hesketh 75 H. Parkes 75 Jos. Harding T. Seed 75 J. W. Dyke T. Rigby J. Bartley 1

Legh-street Band of Hope. The members of the above held their first concert on Tuesday night, when Mr. J. Bailey presided over a large audience. Selections were given by Miss Turner's quartette party. Miss Cruikshanks sang " Goodbye " and " The children's home " which were well received. Miss D. Turner sang " Down the vale " very effectively. Mr. H. Foster rendered a cornet solo, Miss B. Cotton played " The robin's return," while Master Bert Spruce was heard to great advantage in " A visit to London." A most success- ful evening was brought to a close by the humorous , dialogue "Lodgings to let."

Methodist Free Church, Buckley-street.. ,

The anniversary services in connection with the above church were held on Sunday when the preachers was Mr. B.Richardson, of Liverpool. There were largo congrega- tions and in the evening the choir, under the leadership of Mr. N. Cook, gave an anthenineutitlecl " Daughter of Zion." Miss M. Plinston presided at the organ.

===== 0002601_19061110_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
Bold-street Wesleyan Church.

Great interest was aroused by the visit to Warrington on Sunday of the Rev. T. Galland Hartley, chairman of the Liverpool District. Large congregations assembled at the Bold-street Wesleyan Church and the rev, gentle- man's addresses were listened to with marked attention. Special music was interpreted and in the morning the choir ably rendered the anthem " 0 Saviour of the world " (Goss) whilst in the evening they were success- ful in the anthem" Come unto Him " ((loaned). Confirmation Service.

One hundred and eighty-nine candidates were pre- sented at a confirmation service conducted by the Bishop of Liverpool at St. Paul's Church on Sunday afternoon. The candidates from St. Paul's numbered 52 males and 79 females, from St. Barnabas' seven males and lii females, from St. Peter's eight males and eleven female and from St. Ann's nine males and seven females. There was a large congregation. Addressing the candidates, his lordship advised them to walk by faith, and to nut God first in business, and in their amusement. God loved them to be happy and to laugh. He who made the birds to sing and the lambs to play in the meadows loved to see His creatures happy, but their amusements should be of an innocent character.

===== 0002601_19061110_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church.

At this church on Sunday morning the, Rev. John Gunnell preached from the words : " Wait on the Lord ; be of good courage, and he shall strengthen thine heart ; wait, I say, on the Lord " (Psalm xxvii., 14.) At the evening service the pulpit was occupied by the Rev. E. J. Gawne who based his discouse on the text " What wilt thou that I shall do unto thee? And he said, Lord, that I may receive my sight." (Luke xviii., 41). On Monday evening there was a devotional meeting of the Wesley Guild and Mr. Billingham gave an address on " The prayers of Christ." considerable interest centres in a debate which will take place at the Guild meeting next Monday on" The disinterested manage- ment of public houses." Mr. C. E. Parker has promised to address the meeting.

===== 0002601_19061110_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
Baptist Missionary Anniversary.

The missionary anniversary services were held in the Baptist Church, Latchford, on Sunday, the preacher in the morning being Mr. Stephen Ackerley, of Latchford, and in the evening the Rev. E. C. Smyth, of China. During his addess Mr. Smyth explained that hs was in China during the Boxer rising and had to fly for his life. He also otflciaied for six months as a surgeon in the navy on the Barfleur under Admiral Sir George Bruce, by whom he was most, kindly treated. An interesting account of the work in China was given. The offertories were in aid of the Baptist Missionary Society.—At the Baptist Church Golborne-street, Mr. John Harrison, of Liverpool prbached at the morning and evening services to large congregations. In the afternoon, the annual missionary meeting was held, when Mr. 13. Few presided over a fair attendance. An address was given by the Rev. E. C. Smyth and proved interesting and instructive.

P.S.A. Meetings. There was again a fair attendance at the weekly meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A., when Mr. R. Mills pre- sided and spoke upon " The resurrection." Mr. Blane, of Carrington, took for his subject " Individuality." Unity, he said, was strength, whether political unionism or trades unionism, but to have a strong union there must be strong individuality. If young men were healthy and strong they could look forward to a strong union of forces, to fight the evil influences around them. They looked for great ideals in the leaders of men, to guide, control and influence the great army of followers. Mr. Joseph Owen took part in the meeting, and :kir. W. H. Payton presided at the organ. Two new members were welcomed, making a, total of 496. An open meeting of the Buckley-street P.S.A. was held on Sunday. Mr. Edwin Plinston presided, there being a capital attendaace of members and friends. Mr B. Richardson gave an address to the young people relating some of his experiences in the slums of Liver- pool. Miss L. Fairhurst sang two capital solos, " 0 dry those tears," and " For the children's sake," Miss M. Plinston being the accompanist. Mr. W. Shaw read the afternoon's lessons.

===== 0002601_19061110_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
St. Paul's Church. Dedication of New Vestry.

The new vestry of St. Paul's Church was dedicated by the Bishop of Liverpool on Sunday evening. The usJal office of Evening Prayer was gone through, and after the third collect, the Bishop standing near the inside door of the vestry, offered dedicatory prayers. In the course of a short address he expressed his pleasure at the marked improvement in the appearante of the church. He should like to say as their bishop how thankful he was that the Vicar had seen his way to remain amongst them. Had he accepted the Sheffield living, not only would his departure have been a loss to the parish, but he ventured to say that it would have been a loss to the diocese. He was thankful the Vicar had seen his way to continue his work among them, and that he could still number him as among the clergy of the diocese of Liverpool. Lastly, the Bishop said he threw it out as a suggestion only that the congregation ought to have in view the need of a second curate. He knew, that at the present time it was impossible to provide the funds, but he trusted that at no distant period it would be practicable to obtain a grant from some great church society, or, perhaps, from some local society. With more than 8,000 people in the parish, half whom belonged to the Church of England, with their frequent services, and the mission church to look after, and with the Vicar as chaplain of the Workhouse justice could not possibly be done to the work with one curate only. .He knewthat such an addition to the staff would need still greater self-denial, but he believed that when the right moment came they would help the diocese and that the diocese would help them to bring about that most desirable result.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON

NEWTON URBAN COUNCIL.

- - The monthly meeting of the Newton-in-Makerfield t rban Council was held on Monday eveng at the Earles- own Town Hall. Mr. T. Ormand presided, and there were also present :—Dr. Patterson Messrs. W. Thomp- son, L. E. Pilkington, N. Siddeley, F. Harwood, M. Seton, T. Ritchie, E. T. Evans, J. Naylor, E. L. Bent, C. B. F. Borron, G, F. Clarke,'C. Cole (clerk), A. Bowes (surveyor), R. T. Surtees (water engineer). The Council extended their congratulations to Mr. Thompson on his restoration to health.—The Chairman congratulated Mr. Clarke upon his election to the Liverpool City Council, and said he would like to have an assurance from him that his new office would not interefere with his position on the Newton Council.—Mr. Clarke readily gave that assurance. The committee minutes were approved. The Gas Committee recommended than the application of the lamplighters for at increase in wages be refused. The Sanitary committee renewed the slaughter-house licences of Messrs. R. Jones and J. Jackson and of the Co-operative Society.— I The General Purposes Committee resolved that applica- tion be ma,de to the Lord Chancellor for the appointment of additional magistrates for this division,and that in the event of a list of names having been placed before him I for consideration the names of the following gentle- men be submitted for appointment having regard to the services rendered by them to the township : Messrs. T. Ormand, C. B: F. Borron, W. Collingwood and E. T. Evans.—lt was , decided that application be made to the Local Government Board for their sanction to the borrowing, for a term of seven years of a sum of £1,500 to defray the cost of carrying out I certain private street works. HEALTH OF THE TOWN. In his report upon the health of the town during the last quarter, Dr. Watkins states that there 145 births- -81 males and 65 females—and 58 deaths. The birth-rate was 30.49, against 23.19 in the preceding quarter, and 25.93 in the third quarter of last year. The death-rate was 12.11 ; last quarter it was 13.99, and in 1905 16.25. The deaths of children under one year of age was 23 •, between one and five, 9 ; between 5 and 15, 1 ; 15 and 25,4 ; 25 and 65, 11 ; and 65 and upwards, 10. The causes of death were :—Whooping cough, 1 ; diphtheria 1 •, diarrhma, 14 ; puarperal fever, 1 ; pulmonary and phthisis, 4 : other tubercular diseases, 4 ; cancer, 1 ; pneumonia, 4 ; alcoholism and cirrhosis, 1 ; premature birth, 3 ; diseases and accidents of parturition, 1 ; heart diseases, 7 ; accident, 1 ; other causes 15. The deaths of children, under five years of age equalled 5517 per cent of the total deaths ; under the age of one pear, 1575 per 1.000 of the births. The deaths frbm zyrnotic disease-16—were as follows :—Whooping cough, 1 ; diptheria and membranous croup, 1 ; diarrhoea (epidemic), 14. Of those deaths eleven were of children under the age of one year and four between the ages of one and five. The zymotic death-rate was 3.31. Eighteen cases of infectious disease—diptheria, nine ; erysipelas, four ; scarlet fever, four ; puerperal fever, ode—were notified, the infectious sick rate being 3275 as compared with 8.14 last quarter and 1264 in 1905. Five cases of pulmonary phthisis were notified voluntarily. The quarter had been the healthiest since he was appointed medical officer, and the township had been more free from infectious disease than it had for several years. PLAYING WITH FIRE.

CORONER BRIGHOUSE AND LEGISLATION. An inquest was held on Tuesday at Warrington, by Mr. S. Brighouse (county coroner), concerning the death of Archalaus Vincent Redhead,the three-year-old son of Mary Hannah Redhead, a widow, residing at 4, Canal- street, Earlestown.—At seven o'clock on Tuesday, October 23rd, Mrs. Redhead went to see Dr. Valentine for some medicine for her sick child. She left three of f her own children and a neighbour's child in the house. On her return she found that Vincent had been burned, and she at once called in Dr. Valentine. The follow- ing day the boy was removed to the Warrington Work- house hospital, where he succumbed to severe burns about the face and neck on Saturday. The children informed Mrs. Redhead that Vincent Ind theneighbour's child had been playing with paper at the fire, and that Vincent's clothing became ignited. There was no guard round the fire, Mrs. Redhead said, but she added that there was very little fire in the grate when she went out. She was away half an hour.—Coroner Brighouse, speaking generally with regard to deaths caused in similar circuttstances, said this was every important matter which ought to engage the attention of the Legisture. He did not want people to think that the coroners of England were asleep in j the matter. They had considered it, and were about to confer with the council of the N.S.P.C.C., so as to ' see whether some legislation could not be adopted , which would reduce the number of deaths of young children from suffocation whilst sleeping with adults, and from scalds and barns inflicted throltgh unprotected fires. Perhaps the jury would be appalled to know that in 1901, 2,200 children died from the causes mentioned. That showed an enormous increase in comparison with previous years. Life was a very precious asset to the community, and ought to be protected just as ranch as money was. With regard to Mrs. Redhead, he recollected holding an inquest on her husband, and he did not want to add to her grief. She had a large family to look after, and had one child ill, and was bound to go to the doctor. That sort of people had not all the conveniences, com- forts and money to enable them to have the protection that others enjoyed. November bad arrived, and now there was going to be a regular series of burning cases until the end 'of April, and it made him (the Coroner) quite sad and ill when he came to Warrington and other places in‘South-West Lancashire and held inquests on those poor children who were burned to death.—The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death."

St HELENS BURNING TRAGEDY.

Mary Josephine M'Dermott, aged nine months, of 15, Bramwell-street, Sutton, St.Helens. met with a terrible death on Tuesday night. The police report that the child was left in the house by her mother, who went to a neighbouring shop to make a purchase. The child was at that playing on the hearth-rug with her little brother, and about ten minutes after the mother had left the little boy went to the shop and told her that the baby was on fire. She immediately returned, and found the child had received terrible injuries, death , ensuing an hour later.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. [CarusTrAN.—The matter mentioned in your lette is not suitable for publicltion in the press.—ED. "Et."

The New Vicar of Bray. Sir,—lt is said of a famous New York financier, that on sending forth his son into the world he summarised the paternal advice with these words : " Make money— honestly if you can—but make it." These words have been recalled to my mind by the recent municipal elections. Certain gentlemen seem to have based their actions on a somewhat similar motto "Keep out the Non-traders—honestly if you can—but keep them out."

Kfter reading certain circulars issued on the eve of the poll in Latchford Ward it would seem that the said gentlemen have had to seek refuge in the alternative, Why this deadly fear of the Non-Trading Party? Why must their leader be kept out by fair means or foul ? I congratulate the burgesses of Latchford on the adaptability of their Liberal representative. This benign old gentleman has indeed " in his time played many parts " and in comparison with him our old friend die Vicar of Bray was a paragon of consistency. We await with interest the municipal drama to be produced in Latchford next November in which the above gentleman will play " lead." What that " lead " will be is wrapt up in the mystery of he future,—Yours truly, November Bth 1903.

The Liberal Party and the Municipal

Elections.

Sir,--I am glad to see tha in list Saturday's "Examiner "you spoke out strongly about the conduct of certain Liberals in the municipal elections, and about the mismanagement of affairs by the party generally. Plain speaking has been wanted for a long time, and in my opinion the official ranks of the party will have to be entirely reconstituted before there is any hope of Liberalism making headway in this town of monopolies, vested interests, and petty jealousies. The policy of the Liberal party at the municipal elections seems to consist entirely of having a smack at the Non-Traders, and the results each November have been ghastly. Take last week's election for instance ; the two Liberal nominees were both defeated, and another Liberal who opposed a Non-Trader as an "rode- pendent " suffered a similar fate. The Liberals have been on the wrong track for a long time and never in ray recollection have their interests been sacrificed so foolishly as they are to-day. I am quite sure that the rank and file of the party does not hold with the conduct of the official leaders. Why should the party be set against the Non-Traders at every opportunity ? The latter have done an immense amount of good, and if they are careful not to ride the high horse too much they should have the power to do much more yet in the interests of the ratepayers. As a matter of fact, Mr. Editor, the Liberal Party ought to be working with the Non-Traders instead of fight- ing them. If the present suicidal policy is continued, it is only a matter of time before the rank and file of the party rise in their numbers and make a clean sweep at headquarters. Let us have some honest politics and some straightforward leadership, in place of bias and intrigue.—Yours etc.,

A RANKER.

The Church .Meeting at Parr Hall

Sir.—l atnione of those individuals who like to hear all sides of a question. Feeling therefors interested in the Education Question I hied myself to the meeting held in Parr Hall last Tuesday. Well I enjoyed myself for to say the least of it Archdeacon Madden's speech was distinctly humorous and I was glad to find that even a clergyman can be jocular at times. But alack what little information the public who attended had given to them ! And what attempts to throw dust in their eyes ! It was presumably on behalf of the children, the dearchildren,the spiritual condition of the children,that they protested against the Government's Education Bill, but at the same time in the main the speeches were about the Church being robbed. They said the Government desired to confiscate the schools. In fact one speaker said the Bill" was a modernized from of the 40 thieves rob- bing parents of their rights,churchmen of their property and Christian Englishmen of their religious liberty." Fancy Church of England men using language like that ! Surely they should be the last people in the world, who should talk about " robbery " or " confiscation." Do they forget the history of their own church? Did they not themselves "confiscate and rob" the Roman Catholic Church and transfer the churches and land to their own particular religous use ? But, however.putting that one side is it not at least a travesty of words to say that the present Bill, seek to confiscate the Church schools? Why it empowers Local Authorities to purchase on fair and equitable lines. Surely professedly Christian men should not attempt to mislead people. Then at the meeting the "political Nonconformist came in for a good deal of hostile criticism. Fancy Churchmen who above all in the respect live in a glass house, throwing stones. Why the Church of England is apart from the House of Commons and of the Lords, the greatest political institution in the country. Is not Church and State their great political war cry ? And to make matters worse instead of "trusting the people " they are beseeching and petitioning the House of Lords to throw out the Bill. Is it not a disgrace and a scandal that they should appeal to a House that is not the elect of the people but is self-elected ? Surely they must be conscious at times that they have no right to attempt to interfere with the "people's will" and one wonders where they get their notion of "justice and equality" from. There was an expression of the Archdeacon's whiGh I must point out was a very serious one. He cannot understand the objection to the Catechism. He says it contains the unadulterated Word of "God." Now it is because the Catechism is "adulterated" and not pure and contains untruths, that the Nonconformist objects to his children being taught from it. I purpose going into this question of the Catechism in a future letter.— Yours etc.. FREE CRITIC.

===== 0002601_19061110_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
The Rector and Ritual.

Sir,—The report of the recent Liverpool Diocesan Conference forms interesting and instructing reading from many points of view. I am particularly interested in that portion describing the discussion of the report of the Royal Commission on Disorders in the Church. During that discussion, which turned upon Prayer Book I Revision, the Rev. Canon Willis, Rector of War- rington, is reported to have stated in opposing an amendment moved by the Rev. Canon Woodward, D.D., that :—" He thought the time had not come for any change. He earnestly longed, whenever the time came, that there might be a new revision of the Prayer Book on the lines of the Prayer Book of 1549." Now, sir, if he he correctly reported, this is candour which every Protestant Churchman will admire, while profoundly differing from the sentiment expressed—and while I have the highest respect for the Rector of War- rington and his colleagues in the town,—l as earnestly hope that Warrington Churchmen and Churchwomen will remember what a revision "on the lines of the Prayer Book of 1549 "involves. Being the first of a series of revisions when the English Church was shaking off the thraldom of Popery, it contained very much ritual and doctrine which rightly belonged to the Romish Church. Among other things it may be mentioned that the word "altar " was retained, hence the eastward position of the celebrant, the frequent use of the sign of the Cross, auricular confession, reservation of the Sacramental Elements. In the Baptismal Service Chrism and Exorcism were retained. Extreme Unction and prayers for the dead also found their place in this book—and this it is hoped will be the lines of the new Revision when it comes. The general tone end spirit of the book so far as Protestant Reform was concerned is indinted by the fact that in 3 years it was necessary to proceed further, hence the book of 1552 in which these things were expunged. Now, if energies' influence and teaching m result from "earnest hopes " then it is easy to see in what direction many are being led, for the Rector is by no means alone in this view.

The result of tha Elizabethan compromise with its latitude of interpretations is clearly seen in the fears and suggestions of such men as Burnett. Stillingfleet, Tillotsond and Beveridge of the commissions which sat in 1699, and the state of things which brought this last commission into existence. Is it any wonder that many of the laity dread the " Shadow of the Priest "in the English Church, and look askance at her suggestions in the matter of Religious Education? Let none, after this ask the reason why the little Protestant Church in Bold-street exists and flourishes. —Yours &c.,

FRANK VAUG,HAN. Emmanuel Church, Warrington. November Bth. 1900. -- Mr.. W. P. Rylands and the Non-Traders.

• Sir, —I must publicly protest against the reel:less way in which the new Chairman of the Conservative Associa- tion has opened his public career. c In last Saturday's "Examiner." I challenged Iklr. Rylands to find any evidence whatever in support of his statement that "it was difficult to avoid the suspicion that the Non-Trading party is a political organization working directly against the Conservative cause." I now repeat my challenge. But in a speech at the Conservative Club after the declaration of the polls on Thursday week, Mr. Rylands seems to me to have lost his head altogether. He said that " just because Councillor Wilson had taken a certain office to which it was well known and under- stood there was a small emolument attached—and Councillor Wilson acted exactly as other Mayor's auditors had done in taking the small remuneration— he was vituperated by the Non-Trading party in a manner which was absolutely scandalous." Mr. Rylands knew on ought to have known when he made this statement that Councillor Wilson had not acted exactly as other Mayor's Auditors had done since • the Non-Traders entered the Council in November,

11902. From that time, as Mr. Rylands knew or ought Ito have known, no Mayor's auditor has received a fee until a few weeks ago when for the financial ye r end- ing March, 190 G, Councillor Wilson accepted £3O. Mr. Rylands knew or ought to have known that the Non- Traders were al-solutely bound by the underlying prin- ciples of their movement to bring Councillor Wilson's act before his constituents and to endeavour to prevent his return, and this entirely apart from the question of his political views. For Mr. Rylands' assertion that Councillor Wilson "was vituperated by the Non-Trading party in a manner which was absolutely scandalous," I have to deliber ately say that there is absolutely no foundation in fact I have gone through my speeches and my addresses and the speeches of Councillor Pemberton (he and I are the only Non-Traders who have publicly referred to the subject) and I affirm that beyond a most fair and necessary statement of the facts and an explanation of the public grounds of our opposition to Councillor Wilson's re-election, not a single word of personal or improper censure of Councillor Wilson has been uttered by any - Non-Trader. I may add that Councillor Wilson himself, in the most frank and friendly way, assured me, during the poll and at the counting of the votes, that he realized and respected my motive in standing against him, and he publicly and cordially thanked me for the friendly and honourable way in which I had conducted the contest throughout On reading in Saturday's papers the gratuitous attack. of Mr. Rylands on the Non-Trading party I at once (on the 3rd instant), wrote to him requesting chapter and verse for his cruel calumny ; on the receipt of his reply I will ask you to publish the correspondence.— Meanwhile, I am, JAMES DODDS, (Chairman, Warrington Non-Trading Association.) • November, 9th, 1906.

Mr. C. E. Parker and Latchford Election. Sir,—Mr. Dodds in a letter in your last Saturday's issue says, `.` the Chairman is opposed to what is known as illegal and immoral trading by members of the Council with the Corporation,if he will add to this the receiving of profit or payment in any way whatever, and will induce his party in Warrington to adopt these principles,and if the Liberal Chairman will do the same, the occupation of the Non-Traders, like Othello's will be gone." . .

I 1 I am right in concluding that Mr. Dodds has the writer in mind when he speaks of the Liberal "Chair- man," and if any word of mine would be likely to help to restore municipal peace, allay bitter feelings, and heal the dissensions which have been made by this unhappy controversy; it would be no small pleasure to speak that word. It surely is scarcely necessary, either personally, or las representing the Liberal Party to endorse the view of the Conservative Chairman in expressing opposition to illegal and immoral trading. We also endorse and approve the moral teaching of the ten commandments. Where there is room for diversity of opinion is in defining what immoral trading is. Mr. Dodds appnars to assume that the principles of morality are coil- travened if anyone in the Council receives payment for any service he may render. And here we at once come to a point where, with a rigid adherence to the prin- ciples of morality, there is room for differences of opinion. I refer to the concrete instance which induced. the Non-Traders to challenge the re-election of Mr. Wilson in the Latchford Ward. Mr. Wilson when elected three years ago offered his services to the electors, these services were those of an ordinary Councillor, and are well-known. Mr. Wilson is 'asked to undertake a special duty which involved special knowledge, and a considerable expenditure of time and thought. The discharge of these special duties in times past has been remunerated by the fee of £3O. It matters not if some contend that the amount is not properly appraised, we are not dealing with amounts, but with the principle of payment for special services. It appears manifestly unjust that anyone like myself, having various interests in the Borough, abd the payments I have to make in the form of rates upon these interests, being dependent upon the wisdom and efforts of the men who in the Town Council manage my interests ; that I should grudge the legitimate payment of any Councillor who undertakes special work, or that I should withhold my appreciation of the sacrifice which Coun— cillors make in the discharge of these important public duties. Indeed.' would go further and say that if Coun- cillors demanded reasonable payment for ordinary services I should be bound in justice to acknowledge such a claim. There are no doubt objections which can be raised against payment of members, whether parlia- mentary or municipal. It would be inopportune here to go into these, but on the other hand a good deal can be said in favour. My name has been mentioned as one who might be of some little use in the Council. If I choose to reserve my energies for industrial under- takings by which I profit, it would be unworthy to grudge remuneration to those who in the affairs ot the borough do work in which f ought to take my share. Had I leisure to take part, the contentious atmosphere, and the tendency to waste time over personal trifles, would suffice to make me shrink from entering an environment in which I would continually chafe. The danger which Warrington in the future has to face in its local parliament is that there will be a drtarth of men of business training, or practical knowledge of industrial problems, and we may get the Council Chamber occupied by representatives of particular interests. Such by accepting any ticket (be it Non-Trading or any other) which appears to be the winning one for the moment, go into the Council for the purpose of obstructing any action for the good of the whole community, if such action is likely to dis- place their particular interest from some position or privilege or power. This will involve the degradation of the Council, the lowering of its personnel, and the paralysing of all efforts for the public good. Believing thoroughly in the morality of adequate payment for services rendered, just as much as I do in the morality in the payment of any other debt, had I been an elector in the Latchford Ward, and looked upon the point raised by Mr. Dodds as the chief issue, I should have voted for Mr. Wilson. By so doing I should have suffered the " abhorrence " and otlier strong language of the local Liberal paper, which appears to think that we should follow our leanings in Imperial politics in an election in which a candidate, who happens to be a Liberal, expressly states that he is not fighting on political grounds, but for a policy with which we do not agree. I should also have come under the stigma of the inference contained in the words of Mr. Dodds in the address to the crowd after the declaration of the poll. He said, "If he had only received ten votes he would have said there were only ten people in Latch- sord who wanted purity in municipal life." The implication is very clear. Mr. Dodds implies that those who worked and voted for Mr. Wison do not want purity in municipal life. The spirit of the Non-Trading agitation, of which the above is a fair sample, assumes a moral superiority of judgment which makes it difficult for them to listen to admonition however gentle. But may I, with all kindness, suggest that they would have accomplished all they desired, at any rate all they ought to have desired, much more speedily if these efforts to establish purity had been governed by the rule that in things doubtful there should be liberty,and in all things there should be charity. If I might venture to suggest a further fault in this controversy I would name the total absence of humour. If we had amongst us a local Gould who could with facile pencil have hit ofy the comic features of the squabble, such a person would have put the borough under great obligation and deserved a remuneration to which I doubt if even the Non-Traders would have objected. This might have saved an exhibition of a great deal of bad temper, might have prevented the strain upon many old friendships reaching the breaking point, stayed the resignation of valued helpers in, several Associations, and lightened and brightened the public life of the Borough with smiles and laughter. It has been a serious affair, almost a dismal one, and it is time that everyone should try to restore the good feel- ing and good humour which of late has been absent from our public life.

Retnrning to the invitation of Mr. Dodds to state my position as President of the Liberal Association I repeat (and in this may I not speak for both Associa- tions ?) we are opposed,and will always oppose, all who. seek to get into the Council with the object—may I use the usual phrase—of grinding their osvn axes. But as Associations v. e are not prepared to adopt the inter- pretation of any body of men, however earnest and honourable they may be, as to the application of any principle they may adopt. In questions outside the leading ideas which actuate us in imperial politics, and I which may to some extent !Tub, to local affairs,our mem- bers claim and rightly claim liberty of thought and action in respect to matters of opinions, such for example as the payment of an auditor. And as far as lam con- cerned I will do what 7 can to safeguard this liberty of our members from attacks from over zealous friends as well as from political opponents. I strongly deprecate any attempt to use the name of our Associdtion or its Executive to support any opinion which may be advo- cated. In political things which are essential I plead for unity, in municipal affairs which are doubtful, for liberty, and in all things for charity. CHARLES E. PARKER, President of the Warrington Liberal Association. Penketh, November 7th, 1.906.

The Cuerden Hall Sale.

The 17th and last day's sale took place on Tuesday afternoon, when there was a good attendance of buyers. On Monday the contents of the butler's pantry (china and glass), kitchen, scullery, cellars, and nursery, and of the servants bedrooms in No. 1. east wine were disposed of, and on Tuesday a quantity of Staffordshire ware, the contents of the tea room, the moat tea house, the lawn tennis houses, as well as a number of carpets, chairs, toilet services, &c. No big price was obtained, but all the lots went off quickly and reasonably. YEARS OF INDIGESTION.

A Salvationist's Acute Sufferings Ended by Bile Beans.

The prevalence of stomach disorders in November is a large measure to the increase in the consumption of food, and the greater strain on the vitality in keeping up bodily beat. The stomach and bowels become slug- gish and cannot cope with the work without assistance. Bile Beans render just that assistance necessary to keep the organs up to concert pitch. In cases of chronic indigestion, also, Bile Beans are always reliable, as is proved in the experience of Ars. Elizabeth Ann Story, of 9, Cross Street, Mount Pheasant, Gateshead. Mrs. Story is a member of the local Salvation Army corps. "Itis 14 years since I became ill." she says. " I had a strange heavy feeling in my side, and I endured agonies from pains in the small of my back and accoss the kidneys. The pains were so bad at times that I could neither sit, lie, nor stand without enduring great suffering. I had absolutely no relish for food, and ate scarcely anything. During the daytime I was unable to do any work, and at night I got no sleep. "I went into two hospitals for treatment, but after I came out the old trouble returned. The weakness hurried on the development of an abscess in my back, but though I was told there was no hope • for me except in an operation I shrank from that. I could not afford to go to any more doctors, and those I had been to had done me do good. "Two years ago I began using Bile Beans and per- sisted in taking, them regularly. Now I feel quite vigorOus and strOng, and am in the best of of health." Bile Beans are invaluable in Novamber, when exposure to damp and fog reduces the vitality. They increase the power of resistance by toning-up the stomach and liver, and restoring healthy functions. All chemists stock Bile Beans, at Is. 1.(1. and 2s. 9d. per box (contains 3 times quantity.)

THE EDUCATION BILL.

WARRINGTON CHURCHPROPLE'S PROTEST. Speeches by Kr—cid—neon_ Madden

and llr. lan Malcolm

The Parr Hall was well filled on Tuesday evening when Churchpeople assembled to protest against the Education Bill which is now before the House of Lords. The meeting was the second organized by the Warring- ton Association of Church of England Schools. Mr. J. Charlton Parr presided, and the principal speakers were the Ven. Archdeacon Sfadden, of Liverpool,and Mr. lan Malcolm. On the platform were most of the local clergy and many prominent laymen. The Rev. James Davies, secretary, announced the receipt of letters of apology for non-attendance from the Rev. James Collins and Alderman Forshaw.

===== 0002601_19061110_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
"VAGUE, SHADOWY RELIGION."

The CHAIRMAN said a Bill had been introduced by the Government and passed by the House of Commons by which religion would be practically excluded from the curriculum of their elementary schools. (Applause.) He knew it was contended that only denominational religion was excluded, but to his mind religion had no heart, no soul, no influence, no substance unless it be denominational, and undenominational religion was to all intents and purposes no religion at all. (Applause). The vague shadowy religion, or so-called religion, which it was proposed to teach, could have little or no influence on the life and conduct of the child, or of the manor woman that the child grew up into. Would their forefathers have fought and died for the sake of their religion, if it had been undenomina- tional, if it had been composed of a few moral maxims and texts culled from the Bible, a few common-place hymns, colourless and vapid, and a few prayers composed by, or uuder the auspices of the Education Committees of the county and municipal councils ?—(applause.)- -a formula of doubtful efficacy, which might vary considerably with the varying complexion, political and religious, of these miscellaneous and ever-changing bodies. Yet that was the so-called religion which in this 20th century it was proposed to inculcate in the elementary schools. If it were not so serious a matter it would be ridiculous and absurd. (Hear, hear.) These were days when it was seriously contemplated that children should not only be educated free, but should have free breakfasts given to them. He was not going to quarrel with this, if it could be managed, but this he did feel, that they would be feeding the little ones in one way and starving them in another. They would give them a free breakfast, but would withhold from them the Bread of Life, the nourishment which their souls required as urgently, as imperatively as their poor hungry bodies needed the material sustenance of the free breakfast. They had a command laid upon them which even their present House of Commons, as a body, would hardly dare to repudiate. " Feed My lambs ' was the Divine injunction, and while they had life and strength, and an arm to raise, or a voice to speak, let them implore, nay, if needful, let them command their Legislature, the Lords and Commons, to pass no Bill, to make no law, which should deprive their little ones of the spiritual food they had hitherto enjoyed,—the religious teaching, based upon the Bible and the Book of Common Prayer, and its corollary, the Church Catechism, which had made England strong and respected throughout the world as a God-fearing, befnoumble, honest people, a people who could be trusted, whose word was their bond, who stood before the world as the avengers of injustice, tyranny and wrong, and the champions of ustice, humanity and liberty. (Applause.) ,The decision rested with them. Would they support the House of Lords in their endeavours tO mend, or, if need be,. to end, this subversive and mischievous Education Bill, or would they be alders and abetters in j inflicting a cruel and irrevocable wrong on the religious and God-fearing community of this country? (Applause.)

THE RESOLUTION. Colonel Bucxxos proposed the following resolution,— "That we, in this meeting assembled, most earnestly protest against the Education Bill and atsrm that no EAucation Bill can satisfy members of the Church of England which does not allow children to be trained in accordance with the faith of their parents, in school hours, by teachers who believe what they teach and who are qualified to teach it." (Applause.) In other words, they demanded that the compulsory educa- tion in this country should continue to be religious as well as secular. Colonel Buckton traced the growth of our educational system and contended that prior to the passing of the 1902 Act the Government never paid more than half the annual cost of maintaining any Voluntary school. Alluding to the present Education Bill, Colonel Buckton said it called itself" An Act to make further provision with respect to education in England and Wales " but that was a false description. If they struck out the words " make further provision " and substituted " abolish religions instruction" the description would be correct: " An Act to abolish Religious Instruction with respect to Education in England and Wales " (Applause.) Owing to its peculiar constitution, the present Govern- ment allowed the Nonconformists, Unitarians,Agnostics Atheists and other non-Christian political agitators to have it all their own way with the Bill in the House of Commons, but the conduct of the Upper House there- with would increase the national confidence in their hereditary legislators. (Applause.) MB. MALCOLM ON "INJUSTICE AND INIQUITY."

Mr. lAN MALCOL tr in seconding said it would ill become the Church of Church of Edgland, if it were to crawl on its knees and ask for leniency as they had been advised, when what they demanded was a right. (Applause.) They demanded it from a tribunal higher than that of any transient majority ; demanded it from their fellow subjects throughout the country and the Empire, and that justice and equality should pervade in a measure attempting to deal with a problem of such great magnitude as that of religious instruction in their elementary schools. (Applause.) They should acquaint their fellow countrymen throughout the ' country in no voice of passion, but in a voice of calm determination, that they would oppose from the beginn- ing to end any measure which was not more equitable and more reasonable than that which was now being— he was glad to say—battered out of existence in the House of Lords. (Applause.) What had their Church done that her teaching should be dethroned, and the chapel teaching preferred to the seat of the mighty ? The answer was she had displeased the political Non conformist whose dearest wish was to see her disestablished. Continuing the speaker said the ideal of Churchmen was to strengthen, not to weaken the hold that religion had upon the children of every generation, and to urge the State, having taken upon itself the respon- sibilipy of the parent, to make attendance compulsory, to allow the dogma of church and chapel to be taught by the most efficient teechers with all the fervour at

their command—not as a concession to a particular creed but as a protection against a real and a common danger. (Applause.) In that way lay a peaceable and lasting settlement. Failing it they must fight under the banners of religious liberby, with "no taxation with- out participation " and " grievances before supply " as their constitutional battle cry. They would carry the war strenuously into the enemy's camp and demand that if undenominational teaching was to be given in Church schools then they must have Church teaching in Board schools. It was futile to say that dogma was the enemy. Dogma was the keystone of the teaching and strength of Luther and Knox and Wesley. Mr. Gladstone—he wished he was there to help them— (applansa)—once said that to talk about religion and deny dogma was as foolish as to talk about astronomy and deny the stars. It was because they held their dogma so dear, so fundamental, that they would not parley with those who thought ethical instruction a sufficient moral training for children. (Cheers.) ' The resolution was unanimously carried.

ARCHDEACON MADDEN AND THE "MANDATE" I QUESTION. I

Archdeacon MADDEN moved :—" That we, in this meeting assembled, pledge ourselves to further in every lawful way the attainment of the foregoing resolution, and we authorize the same to be forwarded to the Marquis of Ripon, Lord Lansdowne, and the Lord Bishop of Liverpool, for their support." (Applause.) In the course of a characteristically humorous speech the Archdeacon said it had been reported that the agita- tion of Churchmen was having no effect, but what did their opponents Udall when they opened their news- papers on the 2nd November? (Laughter and applause.) They were, as Churchmen, only getting into lighting ' form. They had only flashed their swords. They were I going on to victory. (Applause.) The Archdeacon contended that the rejection of Progressive candidates I in London and the fact that eleven municipal candidates I in Liverpool who a year ago were supporters of the Government now repudiated the Education Bill was an evidence of the public' dislike of the measure. Something had been done in Warrington. At Heywood the people thought it best to turn out the Mayor and ex-Mayor and the Chairman of the Education Committee, and yet 1 there was a mandate from the country for the Bill ! The Government had never received a mandate for it. They had received a mandate to do something for education. They received a mandate to rectify any grievance. Churchmen gave them a mandate for that as much as anyone else. They did not want to have a i grievance upon anyone, their conscience or any other part of their anatomy. (Laughter.) But this was not an Education Bill. as Colonel Backton had told them. It was a theological Bill without any theology. (Loud Laughter.) A large body of Nonconformists were as antagonistic to the Bill as Churchmen were, because they thought they had been betrayed into a false position, and that they were on the slippery slope to secularism. Archdeacon Madden then took Dr. Clifford to task for describing the Archbishop of Canterbury as a" twister," and said the Primate was the most fair-minded Prelate there had ever been in this country. (Applause.) Continuing, he said the present Prime Minister had said that the solution of the diffi- culty was secular education. Other members of the Government, Mr. John Morley, and some of the Labour members, were straight about this matter. Why did they not give them a secular Bill aud let them fight straight ? But instead of that, they had given them a Bill which brought in secularism by the back door, and if they let a man in at the back door and they did not like him they would go out of ths front door. (Laughter.) The Archdeacon then strongly resented the imputation that Churchpeoplc wanted to drive the Bible out of the schools, and said they did not object to Bible teaching but to Bible snippets. (Applause.) They objected to the Bible Cut up into pieces and administered without rhyme or reason. If the Bible was to be taught it must be taught with something like system and reasonable logical sequence. The Catechism was the Bible in a logical simple form, to teach the children the great essential truths of the Bible until they came of age to read the whole Book for themselves. (Applause.) It was information that was wanted, not inflammation, and Churchmen were the people to give it if they were afforded a chance. He hoped the next meeting at Warrington would be a meeting of Noncon- formists to hear the Archdeacon on their grievances. (Laughter and cheers.) Enthusiasm and drudgery would carry them through, and they would carry the country if they were in earnest. It was said that the Government intended eventually to drop the measure, but why not drop it now? (Applause). It was a scandal that in a Christian country they hai in the twentieth century to fight for religious teachihg in their schools. This country would not continue to stand in the forefront of the nations for liberty, righteousness and justice unless the rising generation had its faith and hopes centred not in gold or in the acquisition of mere material things,but in the moral uplifting of the people. The Catechism which was sattutted with the pure and unadulterated Word of God must be taught in their schools. It was the heritage of their children giving them that strength and vigour of character which had made England miuhty and free. (Cheers). MR. WESTBROOK'S CRITICISM.

Mr. H. J. WESTBROOk, in seconding, said that as to the Bill which left the Commons they might say that its paternity was doubtful, its present existence was precarious and its future mysterious. It was the sup- posed child of the Nonconformist conscience and its supposed consort the people's mandate. But the Non- conformist conscience now disowned the offspring, and judging from last week's elections the people's mandate equally repudiated the babe. (Laughter.) Orphaned and homeless,the little monstrosity was now sheltered in the House of Lords,where it was undergoing severe surgical treatment with a view,if possible,of making it present- able for adoption as a child of the State. As introduced, the Bill consisted of 40 clauses—a modernized form of the 40 thieves robbing parents of their rights, Church- men of their property and Christian Englishmen of their religious liberty. The Bill stood as a monument of tyrannical force containing no single provision which would improve our present system of education, but containing from start to finish premeditated provisions for crippling the schools of Churchmen and Roman Catholics and Jews. It gave to Dissenters an allotment of founders' and preference shares at considerably below par value, whilst Churchmen and Roman Catholics and Jews were allotted some ordinary shares at a high premium. Churchmen were bringing into this concern assets to the value of some 13 millions of money, and they insisted upon having a say in the objects and articles of association. They, the men and women,sons and daughters of the old mother Church, declined to worship the brazen image the modern Nebuchadnezzar had set up; they refused to have their God dishonoured, refused to have fundamental Christianity taught by fundamental unbelievers, refused to exchange their Creed and Catechism and Prayer Book, which contained the Churchman's simple Bible teaching, for the schemes and codes of the local education authority. (Applause.) The resolution was carried unanimously. THANKING tHE SPEAKERS.

Mr. R. T. FAraccouGH proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Parr for presiding, and spoke of him in very eulogistic terms ; also a vote of thanks to the other speakers. The Rev. B. PERCIVAL seconded, and this motion was also carried. .

The meeting closed with the singing of the National Anthem.

TRAVELLER'S ALLEGED EMBEZZLE3LENT.

At the Warrington Police Court, on Tuesday, Harry Parrott, commercial traveller, of Moseley, Birmingham, was charged with feloniously embezzling two sums of money amounting to £IO7s. 6d. belonging to the British Soap Powder Company, Warrington. Mr. F. G. Taylor prosecuted, and Mr. A. Browne defended.—lt was stated 1 by Mr. Taylor that Parrott was employed as a traveller by the company. It was his duty to take orders and collect the accounts. On the 21st May he received an order from a grocer named John Robert Meake at Aston Birmingham, and was paid £3 58. in settlement of the account. Instead of giving one of the company's official 1 receipts, a traveller's card of the company was tendered and signed by the prisoner as a receipt. The money had never been received by the company. Another account amounting to .E 5 2s. 6d. was received by the prisoner from another Birmingham 1 grocer named Dewsbury, which also had never been accounted for to the comp my. Several other cases were quoted by Mr. Taylor where prisoner had collected small accounts but had not accounted for the moneys. —Several witnesses were called in support of this state- ment, but in cross-examination by Mr. Browne it was stated that prisoner told them that 119 could not get his commission, and he had been instructed by the manager and a director to look after himself in getting his commission.—The accused was committed to the I sessions, bail being allowed.

Ashton-under-Lyne Co-operative Society has decided not to stock Trust soaps. Irlam District Council has passed plans for substantial extensions to the already large Co-operative Wholesale Society's Soap Works at Irlam. Dry soap will be made in the new premises.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

[By " Ovalist."]

Warrington re-established themselves in the favour of their supporters an Saturday by successfully accounting for Wigan on the Wilderspool ground. A victory was expected:for the "Wirepullers " have been invincible at home this season, and up to Satur- day's match had scored over 30 points in each home engagement. in Wigan they found opponents more their own calibre, and had nothing to snare through- out the gadle, though thd final score was the comfort- able- one of 12 points to 5.

There was a record crowd for the season of about 6,000, notwithstanding the absence of such " stars " as Leytham and Jenkins (Wigan) and ,Isherwood and Lees (Warrington). Capable substitutes were forth- coming, and a fast exciting game was witnessed. There was a lack of polish about the movements of both skies, and neither was seen at its best, but on the whole the football was interesting. , Wigan Were very aggressive from the start, and it was a stroke of luck which gave Fish the opportunity to send Dickenson over with a good try. The chance cams to the right man and it was made the most of, but the visiting full back must be counted somewhakunlucky in failing to stop the movement. Play travelled rapidly fram one end of the field to the other after this, and if any- thing Warrington had rather the worst of matters. Fish once, when hard pressed, knocked the ball dead with his fist, and was penalised in such easy position that Wigan landed a goal. The judgment was much questioned, and a five yards serum would have been a fairer decision. In successive matches, desperate defensive measures have cost Warrington valuable points.

• The second half of the game opened with Warring- ton only leading by a single point, but a couple of penalties against the Wiganers soon changed the aspect of affairs. Fish obliged with a goal each time and gave his side a useful lead. Wigan strove hard and got possession from the scrummages almost every time, but their combination was not effective. Almost the only time Warrington heeled out in the second half, the backs passed beautifully and Fish took play to the line, where Harmer scored and Preston kicked a goal. Just before time Wigan got some reward for their exertions, Green scoring a try at the corner.

The cOmplaints which have been made against the Warrington forwards were amply justified on Satur- day,when they were hopelessly beaten for possession by a much lighter pack. It was really remarkable to see the regularity with which the ball came out on the Wigan side. Apparently they packed differently from Warrington, but what the secret was it could not be d iscovered from the grand-stand., The only consolation Warrington had was that their opponents frequently got penalised for being rather to) smart at the scrummage. There is something radically wrong with the scrummage work of the locals. The men are both strong and clever but they cannot get the ball with the necessary frequency. -Why, on Saturday if they had had it half the number of times tha Wiganers had they most have won easily, instead of being kept largely on the defensive and in danger all the time.

An interesting feature of the match was the duel between" Bucky ' Green and Fish, who faced each other on the wing. As in the sprint race last year, the Warrington man had the best of matters ; though trained nothing like so fine as his opponont. Fish is farmer-like in build at present, and would do with a dozed lbs. avoirdupois off at least. Still he can run and dodge better than anycne else yet, but he has not the same inclination for work that he once had. It is a rare thing to see Fish follow up now, and yet at one time he could capture a full back as easily as he could dodge one. Brooks is much the more resolute of the two and misses nothing for want of running and trying.

Dickenson took Isherwood's place at left centre,but was best on the defence. He has not the capacity for making an opening that " Danny " has, but at breaking up the combination of the other side he is splendid. Windsor Jones i;49t severely handled once or twice by the lengthy Warrington man and was glad to get rid of the leather. Jordan did not do so well as expected at half back,though he had, of course, few chances. Still Warrington would have made much more headway with Lees at the base of the scrummage. Berry started unsteadily but improved, and in the second half did some fine defensive work. His worst fault is in waiting for the ball to bounce sometimes when be could reach it earlier with an effort. Mason was safe as ever on the Wigan side, but the halves Anderson and Thomas —made less headway than usual and frequently hampered their own centres. Green and Treharne were both smart wings, but found their masters on the home side. The Wigan forwards were brilliant !

-- In their last match or two Warrington have gone back, to some extent, to kicking instead of passing. I hope they will abandon the punt to-day and go in for the handling game. If they do they are certain to beat the Hornets handsomely. Both forwards and backs must pick up, run, and pass at every oppor- tunity. These tactics non-plussed all visitors to Wilderspool, and will prove just as successful away if persisted in. Against the Hornets, Warrington should have possession much more frequently than was the case last week.

Hockey.

Last Saturday Stockton Heath received a visit from the Northern team, a side which, prior to being defeated by Timperley the previous week, had not been beaten for two years, and as all the hockey world now knows, our local eleven were victoious by two goals to one. Both goals for Stockton Heath were scored by W. H. Banks, who played a fine game, and who, provided he maintains his present form, should have more than an autside chance of being chosen for the county during the present season. S. Baxter also showed good form, his stick work being tine, but he would do well to feed his wing man rather more than he does at present.

The defence was very sound if not brilliant, each man working hard and never leaving the Northern forwards alone. Perhaps special mention should be made of E. E. Woods, who played one of the finest games he has ever played, and that is saying a lot. It must not be thought that the Northern team took their beating lying down. Seldom have I seen such desperate yet failing attacks made right up to the Anal whistle. Northern were best represented by Varvvick at inside I left and their goalkeeper.

It must be very gratifying for the old organizers and pioneers of the Stockton Heath club to know that the club they so keenly fought for is now able to hold its I own with any club in the North of England. Why 'we have it on tile authority of the" Daily Mail" that "Stockton Heath are a good side."

The second eleven were rather poorly represented at Northern (Crosby) and as a result were beaten eight goals to three. The goals for Stockton Heath *ere scored by C. W. Walker (2) and Linnel (1). I fail to understand why the second do not do better, and think the players might rally round captain Hawtin more than they do. The Club is fortunate in having such a man as Mr. Hawtin, who seems to take defeat with a cheerful face, so long as he thinks he can coach men for the first eleven.

This week the club play Altrincham ; the first team should win, but the second may and some difficulty in following suit. The following teams have been chosen to represent Stockton Heath :-Ist tat home) on Arpley Cricket Gronnd :—R. G. Pierpoint (goal) ; T. Pierpoint (Capt.) and E. E. Woods (backs) ; R. Hallows, Eric Fairclough and J. D. Hatton (halves) ; H. Dolan, W. Richardson, W. H. Banks, S. Baxter, and C. Smith (forwards). Referee, W. Carter, Esq. Bally off, 2.30 prompt.

2nd team (away) :—W. H. Sutton (goal) ; P. B. Hawtin (Capt.) and B. Silcock (backs) ; H. W. Guest,E. Jones and J. Houghton (halves) ; 12 Pierpoiut, C. W. Walker, A. H. Rathbone, %V. Linnel and A. Hodgkinson (forwards). Referee, N. W. Bellamy, Esq. Train leaves Arpley Station, 1.12 (to Broadheath).

Huyton 1, Sefton 1. Sluth Manchester 0, Timperley 2. E. Pierpoint, the popular skipper of the Stockton Heath team, has been chosen to play for Cheshireagainst Lincoinshire at Brooklands next Saturday. This is the first County honour which has ever come to the Stockton Heath Club, and there is much rejoicing locally at the selection. Pierpoint is a very cool, resolute back and should give a good account of him- self whist' the Penmen.

THE MUSICAL SOCIETY'S CONCERT.

" The Crusadera."

The Warrington Musical Societe made an excellent start with its season's work on Wednesday with a concert which included as its most prominent feature Gade's "Crusaders." Choral members were in unusually strong force, the male-voices especially benefltting by the accession of new members. Nor, as the season opens, is the society's orchestra found to be less strong in point both of number and efficiency. The pity of it all is that so fine a body of musicians should be con- fronted with so beggarly an array of empty benches on an occasion when commensurate public support is the one thing needful to encourage the society's management and to brace up the energies of its rank and file. As it is apparently idle to indulge in repinings,the society would do well to take what consolation it can from the fact that a most excellent concert marked the opening of its session, and from the circumstance that a small audience sheaved such an excess of enthusiasm in regard to it as would have done credit to the discrimination of gathering of ten-fold its numbers. Of " The Crusaders " a most intelligent and praise- worthy performsnce was given. Though the cantata strikes no very profound or deeply impressive note it is full of a certain quality which, often strongly Mendel- ssohnian in flavour, is yet both., powerfully moving and full of dignity. The book abotands in suggestions composer has taken full ch ereCrsuosmadeerfis annedlySirens are engaged, advantage. vtaonnuergpei .c t uAr el os nogf wwi thhi omt thee highly dramatic passages contrasted choral tnhuemr eb earrse inn w tbhi ee bstebo the inclusion of which last mentioned mythical beisgs giving to the story a great fascination. The solo and chorus • Father, from a distant land " struck one as perhaps the most beautiful number in which the chorus is concerned, and curiously here the influence of 'Mendelssohn upon Gade's temperament is most clearly apparent. 'rhe " Spirit music " preceding the appearance of Armida was marked by commendable firmness in the attack by the various sections of voices which are of often heard quite disconnectedly. Nor was the later movement "The wave sweeps my breast," for female voices only,less pleasing and mentorious, trebles and altos here vieing with each other in showing good form. A most effective conclusion of the work is made with the grand ensemble chorus " His head let each Crusader raise," in which the deletion of the somewhat commonplace" Hosianna "phrases may be noted as a distinct gain. The singing of the principal parts in the cantata by Miss Edith Clegg, Mr. James Davis, and Mr. Frederic Austin was all that could be desired. In some solo and concerted portions of the work this capable trio would doubtless have been greeted with disturbing, if compli- mentary, applause had not the continuity of the various movements prevented its manifestation. In the second part of the programme there were some items of a miscellaneous character.Of the separate items of choral singing the performance of the part-song " Since thou 0 fondest alone calls for a comment. Here a great improvement was discernible in a more perfect balance of the various parts which can, of course, be directly traced to the attainment this year of a better numerical proportion in the different sections ; of the voices, lint there were, let it be said, subtle ; qualities shewn in the presentation of the part-song of which members individually may feel justly proud—a oneness of effort in which great expressiveness was I manifest, contributing to quite an ideal ensemble in the rendering of this charming little composition. Mr. Frederic Austin greatly distinguished himself as an exponent of Stanford's suite of Sea Songs for baritone solo and male-voice chorus. It was quickly seen that both the soloist and his sympathetic backers had a congenial theme in ' the recital of those stirring naval adventures which have become the warp and weft, so to speak, of Britain's historical tapestry. Of the use songs comprised in this unique cycle " Homeward bound and "The old Superb " undoubtedly gain most by the juxtaposition of their points of difference. A pathetic sentiment in the text is, in the first of these, wedded to some most telling vocal phrases supported by a fascinating figure in the accompaniment for muted strings, while in the second there is narrated, in spirited solo and chorus, some incidents of so rousing a character as to stir the pulse of the most phlegmatic listener. It is not surprising therefore that, vocalised in the finished manner in which Mr. Austin interpreted it, the concluding song resulted in loud demands upon Mr. Austin to which he ultimately acceded, for its repetition. Miss Clegg sang Bredon hill " with a depth of feeling which was as potent in arousing sympathy as it was highly appropriate to this curious vocal composition—curious in the sense that the com- poser's artistic treatment of what might have been a most monotonous all-pervading pedal-note, approaches closely to a stroke of genius. Mr. Davis had a taking song in Cowen's "When all the world is fair "to which he did ample justice and for which he received a deserved and hearty encore. Schubert's flue overture to " Rosamunde," well- played by a very complete orchestra, formed a welcome prelude to the second half of the programme. It should be mentioned that Mr. F. H. Crossley and Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac. were, as conductor and accompanist, at their accustomed posts, the duties of which they both most ably discharged.
