BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

WOULD-BE BURGLAR CAPTURED.

A smart capture of a would-be burglar was made by Police-constable Edwards, of Warrington, in the early hours of Wednesday. Hearing a noise at the rear of the Brooklands Hotel, Lovely-lane, the officer found an ex-convict, who gave the name of Thomas Hrm3n, hiding in the yard. When being taken into custody, the man made the excuse that he was working wita the nightsoil men. He was taken to the police station, where he made a statement that if the officer had not dropped on him be would have done the job before morning. He was not going to walk about starving.—At the Borough Police Court the magistrates sentenced him to gaol for three months.

RECEIVER_SIIARTLY PUNISHED.

At the Warriicito-n-POiiCe.6,l;rt-osn'W'e-chTeaday,llobert Kirk. Evelyn-street, was charged in two cases with receiving stolen brass from a youth named Enos Woods, who on Monday was convicted for stealing the brass. On Thursday last Woods sold the prisoner 151 b. of brass, for which he agreed to pay 30. per lb., whereas it was really worth lid. per lb. Prisoner who worked at Messrs. Monks, Hall, and Company's works, from where the brass was stolen, knew full well the value of the brass. A previous conviction having been proved against prisoner, the magistrates sent him to gaol for three months. SUCCESSOVL PLEA OF INSANITY. At Chester Assizes, on Thursday, before Nfr. Justice Walton, Edward Spendelow (41), gasfitter, Wurrington, was indicted for unlawfully wounding Margaret Slater, with intent to kill her on October 12th,at Astbury,near Congleton. Mr. Artemus Jones prosecuted. and Mr. W Madden, with Mr. E. Owen Roberts, defended. The storY for tbe prosecution was that prisoner was for ten years in the Army in India, and when he came home he resumed cohabitation with his wife, to whom he had been married for twelve years. Frequent qnarrels arose, and, as a consequence, the couple separated, the wife going to live next door to her sister, Margaret Slater. Prisoner seemed to blame his sister-in-law for the separation, and frequently threatened her. On the day in question prisoner walked in through Slater's front door, and when she saw who it was she ran screaming into the yard. Prisoner followed her and stabbed her four times in the back before she reached the street. The wounds were serious, one of them penetrating the lung. When arrested and charged prisoner denied being in Slater's house.—Mr. Madden, for the defence, called prisoner's mother, whe said prisoner, when a boy of two or three years of age, was in India, where he had sunstroke and enteric fever.—Dr. Peacocke, of Warrington, gave it as his opinion that prisoner was suffering from impulsive insanity.—Dr. Braide of Warrington also said prisoner was insane.—The jury at once found prisoner guilty hut insane, and his Lordship ordered him to be detained at Knutsford until his Majesty's pleasure be known.

BROTHERS IN TROUBLE.

At Warrington Police Court on Monday Thoma I Wood; aged eleven years, of 5-1, Plumpton-street, wife charged with stealing a quantity of brass, value £l, Irons the works of Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co. on Saturdgy evening. Mr. Jenkins (on behalf of Mr. Steel) appeared for defendant, who pleaded guilty. Superintendent Monaghan stated that about eight o'clock on Saturday night a watchman was going through the works when he heard a noise in the fitting shop. On proceeding there he saw the defendant with a little bag which proved to contain two pieces of brass. The boy said he was going to sell them to a man named Kirk at Sankey Bridges. That man was in the employ of Messrs. Monks. Hall and Co., and the police intended to inquire into that side of the matter.—Mr. Jenkins said that statement was true, but the person who was really to blame for the boy's conduct could not,tl unfortunately, be brought before them, it was Mr. Guy Fawkes. That day was the fifth of November, and the boy told him that he stole the brass in order that, with the money it realized,he might buy some fireworks. They had all once been actuated by the insane idea to let off fireworks. He was also informed that this boy was, at times, peculiar in his manner, his mother having stated that he sometimes suffered from absence of mind. The boy was ordered to receive three strokes of the birch.— Enos Woods, an elder brother of the defendant in the preceding case, was charged with stealing 151 b. of brass, value lls. 3d., from the works of Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co., on October 31st.—Superintendent Monaghan said prisoner, was seen by P.C. Woods in connection with the last case, and during a conversation prisoner said he knew nothing about his brother's theft, but volunteered the statement that he had taken some brass. He was employed at the works, awl took the brass whilst working overtime. He said he had I sold it to a man named Kirk, at the rate of 3id. per pound. Mrs. Kirk had been seen in reference to the matter, but she would say nothing about it. Prisoner was birched three years ago for theft. He now admitted that he had been in the habit of stealing brass.—Mr. Jenkins said the redeeming feature of the case was that had not the prisoner told the police of his crime be would not, have been in such a position that day.— Prisoner was fined 20s. and cost (£2).

DEAR Czatmaiic,

As I have just got a few minutes to spare, I don't think I can employ them better than by penning my usual weekly letter to my little relations. "

This week our list of members has swelled again, and I want to see it grow to the extent of 12 each week from now up to Christmas. Last week I asked you all to send me in some original joke, short tale, trick, or anything funny, for my Yuletide letter, and I pro- mised to give three good prizes for the best three things sent in by the end of November. Well, I hope you will do your best, but as I said before, whatever is sent in must be suit- able for entertaining a Christmas Party. Now, besides this, I am willing to give two boxes Qf Fry's delicious chocolates each week to the two member S who send in the best thing in the way of very short tales, a verse of poetry, puzzle,, etc. Well, think I have done my share this week, so I will let some of the Corner con- tributors do theirs, so for another seven days, bye-bye. ' UNCLE NED.

THE MONK'S CHARITY.

There was once a monk who was renowned for his love ofecharity, and, unlike most people, he practised what he preached. He once made a vow that if it was in his power, no beggar should go away from him with an empty pocket. One day this monk decided to go on a pilgrimage to a distant monastery, and on ttie way to visit all the shrinee that he passed. o he went, and faithfully kept his vow, so much so, that when he arrived at a village midway between his own home and the monas- tery he was going to, he had not even a penny- piece to pay for a night's lodging. Suddenly a thought stragk him, " I will go.to Brother John, the cure of this village'," thought he, "and perchance he will help me." Brother John, a goodly: and devout man, readily agteed to give the monk a good supper and a bed. Early the next morning the monk was awakened by the tolling of a church .bell close by. "I will go to early Mass," he exclaimed. When he arrived at the Church door, he saw standing by it a beggar, whose ragged clothes scarcely held together. Out of long habit the monk dived his hands into his trousers pockets, forgettinNlhat-the night before they had been empty, but now, what aid his fingers touch, surely that was money? He pulled out his hands, and disclosed a— crown. This he did three times, every time bringing out the same amount, and handing qt, to the beggar, who seemed overjoyed at his good fortune. Then he went into Church, and soon every- one knew of the wonderful miracle which had been performed that morning.

A child, on being shown the picture of "Daniel in the Lions' Den," was affected to team

"Don't grieve, pet," said the mother," "he was not devoured."

"I'm not crying for that," was the reply; "but do you see that little lion in the corner, mamma? Well, I'm afraid he won't get any, for Daniel is so small he won't go round."

A .MOON SONG.

"0 moon," said the chrren, "0 moon that shineth fair, Why do you stay so far \ away, 80 high above us there? 0 moon, you must be very cold from shining on the sea; If you could come and play with us, how happy we should be!"

"0 children," said the moon, " I shine above your heads., That I may light the ships at night, -when the sun has gone to bed; That I may elbow the beggar-boy his way- ' across the moor, And bring the busy farmer home to his own cottage door.

"0 moon," said the children, "may we shine in your place? They say that I have sunny hair, and I sparkling face, To light the ships and beggar-boys, we greatly do desire; And you might come and warm yourself before , the nursery fire."

" o,children," said the moon, "we have each allotted parts; 'Tis yours to Shine, by love Divine, on happy human hearts; 'Tis mine to make the pathway brisht, of wanderers that roam; • 'Tis yours to scatter endless light, on those that stay at home."

From HORACE TAYLOR.

MR. J. HAVELOCK WILSON AND

MIDDLESBOROUGH.

A rumour has been in circulation in Middlesborough that as Mr. .1. Havelock Wilson, M.P., is a bankrupt, a bye-election will shortly occur in the division. An announcement on behalf of Mr. Wilson was published on Monday, in which it is pointed out that the receiving order' made against Mr. ..Wilson is not equivalent to an adjudication. Mr. Wilson met his reditors on November Ist, and offered them a composition of les. in the pound, and the meeting was adjourned for two weeks to allow the offer to. be submitted to the creditors through the Official Receiver in accordance with the requirements of the Bankruptcy Act. At the next meeting the offer will be considered by the creditors, and no adjudication can take place until December 14th.

VOLATILISING PHOSPHORUS FROM PHOSPHORIC ACID.

Phosphoric acid and the metallic phosphates are generally regarded as being very refractory substances, which resist the action of powerful reducing agents even at fairly high tempera- tures, but Messrs. Janna.sch and Heimann, in a sommunication to the Berichte, have shewn that the whole of the phosphorus of phosphoric acid or a phosphate can be rendered volatile at a temperature below the melting point of glass. This operation is carried out by mixing the phosphate with a solution of cane sugar in mode- rately strong sulphuric acid. On warming, car- bon separates in a very finely divided form, and the mixture is then gently heated in a stream of chlorine, and ultimately, when the fesidue is dry, it is burnt at the same temperature in a current of oxygen. As a result of these re- actions all the phosphorus is carried over in the distillate, and may be obtained in the form of pure phosphoric acid by passing the volatile products into nitric acid. This process, when sufficiently simplified, should prove useful in the analysis of basic slag and other commercially important phosphates.

===== 0002601_19061110_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MYSTERY OF MIGRATION.

Migration is still one of the great mysteries of Nature. In the case of the birds, indeed, it is thought to be partly explained by the assump- tions that the old ltrds lead the way, and that they travel by old land connections. As a matter 'of fact, however, says the Globe, recent researches on bird migration shew that the young migrate apart from the old and that they do not confine themselves to the lines of old land conections. And in the case of other migrations, the suggestion could never be made, for no individual makes the journey more than once. This is the case, for example, with the Norwegian lemming. A-t certain seasons im- mense numbers of these small rodents leave the mountains and journey westwards. Their migra- tion ends in the sea, where they are drowned. And then there is the case of the eel. There are no old eels that have been to sea before to lead the way, for no eel returns again to fresh water. ,In the case of the eel living in the river, of course, it has simply to journey down the river till it reaches.the sea. But there are eels living in various ponds .and ditches, and these have to leave the water and travel across country to the nearest stream. And when the yount• eels come back they find their way not only up the streams, but also across country to the ponds and ditches.. LIGHTNING & LIGHTNING CONDUCTORS. Major Cooper-Key's report on the Sedgwick Gunpowder Mill explosion contains some in- teresting remarks on the protection of build- ings, and especially of explosive factories, from lightning. The report shewed that three build- ings were exploded simultaneously by three separate strokes from the same flash of light- ning. Commenting on this, tbe inspector says a flash of lightning may pass between the clouds and the earth owing to two distinct causes, or rather to two essentially different methods of re- establishing electrical equilibrium. There is the ordinary case in which an electrified cloud in- duces so great a change of opposite sign on the surface of the earth that the intervening resist- ance is overcome by the tension and a spark paPers, and there is the case whore a second cloud is situated between the original cloud and the earth in such a manner that when a spark passes between the two clouds the repelled charge on the underside of the lower cloud is at once set free, and overflows to earth, so to speak. in an overwhelming and comparatively uncontrollable torrent, which often splits up and goes to earth by several different paths simultaneously. The first class is best dealt with by the principle of silent discharge—and it is/ considerably more satisfactory to restore elec- trical equilibrium without violence as much as possible. The second kind of discharge is much more difficult to deal with, for there is no time for preparation. Nothing short of complete en- casement in metal seems to

PROVIDE ABSOLUTE SAPETT for any building. Major Cardew, the late electrical adviser of the Board of Trade, elaborated a scheme for the protection of nitro-glycerine factories in the Transvaal. He iiroposed to place hollow iron pillars at intervals all round the buildings to a height of 401 t., and their lower ends buried about 10ft, deep in the ground. Inside these pillars were to be perforated water-pipes to drench the pillars and the ground underneath. He advised that all buildings should be made of galvanised iron, but, failing that, they should have suspended over them iron netting fastened to the pillars, (End having good electric contact therewith. All water-pipes and masses of metal within the buildings should be connected with the outside system, so that in these masses of metal no charge may be Produced inductively. Everything shbuld be well "earthed." Major Cardew's system, with a little modifica- tion, has been in use eat the Modderfontein Dynamite Factory for some time. Twice has

===== 0002601_19061110_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
LIGHTNING GOT' PAST

the out defences, and discharged within the building, but on both occasions the lightning seems to have struck a pipe outside the protec- ted area, and followed it into the building, thus shewing the importance of insulating all the pipes thoroughly from the actual walls and machinery. Two or more well-earthed conduc- tors should be raised, as high as practicable, a yard or two away from the actual building, or on the screening mounds if these be provided. These should b: connected near their upper ter- minals by means of barked-wire cables. If the conductors are actually in contact with the bild- ing there is an increased risk of a portion of the flash seeking an alternative path. The above principles' are accepted, says Major Cooper-Key, by most modern authorities, but the question has arisen lately as to whether copper or iron is

===== 0002601_19061110_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BETTER METAL

for use as a conductor. To the uninitiated the high conductivity of copper would seem to de- fend its superiority; but Sir Oliver Lodge main- tains that that very conductivity is a defect in that the electric energy is thus dissipated so sud- denly. Iron, on the other hand, from its higher self-inductive properties and consequent loss of conductivity, absorbs the energy gradually, and dissipates it with tar less tendency to " surging -n and 'side-flash." On the other hand, the cables of copper and iron necessary to carry. a lightning charge must have respectively a weight of Oort sad 211 b. per foot. _

THE MAYORALTY.

• ELECTION OF ALDERMAN SMETHURST.

Aldermen and Committees.

THE PUBLICATION OF COUNCIL BUSINESS.

Alderman James Smethurst was at a meeting of the Town Council held at noon yesterday unanimously elected Mayor of the Borough. Considerable interest centred in the proceedings and there was a large attendance of Councillors and members of the general public. The retiring Mayor (Alderman Bolton) presided at the opening of the meeting and there were also present :—Aldermen Tinnion, Pierpoint,Bennett, Evans, Francomb ; Councillors Fulton Smith, Evans, Skelland, Chantler, Sankey, Ashcroft, Wilding, Stott, Cunnington, Ellison, Ball, Steel, Lord, Craik, Clegg, Dr. Joseph, Barton, Duncan,Burton, Toole, Benson, Wilson ; Messrs. J. Lyon Whittle (Town Clerk), A. T. Holloway (deputy Town Clerk), J. Dens (water engineer), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer) J. G. Gornall (medical officer of health), J. Moore Murray (director of Education), and G. F. F. White (magistrates' cleik). Among the visitors present were Mrs. Smethurst, Mrs. Bolton, Rev. W. Bracecamp, Messrs. John White, Robert Pierpoint, and J. H. Smethurst

THE NEW MAYOR.

Alderman FRANCOME said he had very great pleasure in proposing : "That James Smethurst, Esq., one of, the aldermen of this borough, be and he is hereby I elected Mayor for the ensuing year and until the accept- ance of office by his successor." He need not say that Alderman Smethurst had done all in his power during the many years he had been in the Council and the time he had filled the office of Alderman, to give perfect satis- faction and to further the interests of the town. He believed Alderman Smethurst would devote as much time as possible to the duties of his office and that at the end of the year he would have given everybody perfect satisfaction. (Applause.) Alderman Traszoe seconded and said he did so with very great personal pleasure. He thought the position of Mayor of this Borough at the present time was one of vast importance to the whole interests of the rate- payers, of the town, and of the Council. He also thought Alderman Smethurst's election would be a very fitting reward for the great ser- vices he had rendered to the town through the Council and by his association with every local institution, going back for over thirty years, which had had for its object the improvement and betterment and the making happier the conditions of the people of Warrington. (Hear, hear.) They all knew Alderman Smethurst, and they all knew his work, and he felt sure that the Council in electing him to the position would give entire satisfaction to the rate- payers and the townspeople generally. Unfortunately Alderman Smethurst suffered from a very high nervous temperament and it had been said to him (Alderman Tinnion) that probably that fact, would be taken advantage of by some members of the CounciL (Cries of "No.') He (Alderman Tinnion) had always said "No" to that statement, The members of the Council while recognising Alderman Smetnurst's ability would also sympathise with him in any difficulty he might have to overcome through his highly sensitive and highly strung nature and he felt sure the appointment would be the means of every member trying to do his best to smooth away all difficulties and remove all the troubles of the past, if it were possible and he thought it was. If that were the result of their action, he felt sure it would be a great boon and benefit to the Borough. (Applause.) Alderman BENNETT supported and said he had a very high opinion of Alderman Smethurst's qualities. In connection with the work of the town he had given unstintedly of his services in the Council and in many other capacities. On more than one occasion he had headed the list of attendances with the exception of the Mayor for the time being, and no trouble seemed to have been too great for him to undertake if he felt he was doing anything for the benefit of the town. He had • taken particular pride and pleasure in the work of the elec- tricity and tramways commit,tee,and it would be common knowledge to the ratepayers how sedulous he had been to work those great undertakings to the advantage of the town, and that he had assisted to turn a loss into a profit. He had always been willing to do what he could to promote any good cause, and was in the forefront of movements for the prosperity of the municipality. The position of Mayor was no bed of roses ; it was difficult and troublesome. They had been going through anxious , times, and any man who undertook the duties was deserving of the best wishes of the town, and was entitled to every credit for the work he did. He felt confident that Alderman Smethurst would occupy the position with dignity, ability and impartiality, and that in Mrs. Smethurst he would have an efficient and popular help-meet. (Applause). Councillor PEMBERTON supported and said that so far as he and those associated with him were concerned they would give Alderman Smethurst every assistance in their power. He had served under Alderman Smethurst on the Tramways Committee and the alder- man had always treated that committee in a fair and proper manner. If he did the same as Chairman of the Council, he would receive all the support he (the speaker) could give him. The great point was for the Cbairman to be impartial. Councillor TOOLS said he could assure the Council that it would give the greatest pleasure to the body of which he was a member that Alderman Smethurst should attain the highest office that could be conferred upon him by his fellow townsmen. (Applause.) The resolution was heartily agreed to. The proposer and seconder then adjourned to the Mayor's parlour to inform Alderman Sfuelhurst of the decision of the Council. A few minutes later Alderman Smethurst, wearing the Mayoral robes and chain, entered the Council Chamber and took the chair amid applause. Having made the customary declaration of acceptance of office the Mayor addressed the Council as follows : Mr. Alderman Francomb, Mr. Alderman Tinnion, Alder- man Bennett, Councillor Pemberton, Councillor Toole, and gentlemen,—I cannot disguise from you the great sense of responsibility which I feel in accepting the office of Mayor of this important town. lam sore you will make all allowance for the very trying circum- stances with which lam faced. It is no light matter for a young and vigorous man to sit in this chair and to take upon himself the duties and the office of Mayor, and, having regard to my years, I must ask for your forbearance, not only at the present moment but during my year of office. I must thank you very warmly for your unanimons vote, and for the honour you have conferred upon me. It is an honour which T and my family will prize more than any other, and we shall regard it as the greatest honour which can be bestowed upon a citizen by his fellow townsmen. (Applause). While Warrington is not my native town I have lived so long among you as to be entitled to call myself a Warringtonian, and I have grown to love the town of my adoption, I believe, as much as any of those who had the privilege of being born in the town. It is 15 years to-day since I entered the Council, and I have attended 2,1.88 times or 85 per cent. Council meetings; during nine years as magistrate, 893 ; Education Committee, 08: total, 3,157. Warring- ton has made great progress during the last few years. Since I have been a member of the Council the town has acquired its Waterworks,and it has established an Electri- city Works and its Tramway undertaking. The extension of the latter into the township of Stockton Heath has provided greater facilities between the two communi- ties. Warrington has during my time among you celebrated its 50th anniversary of incorporation, and has risen to the dignity of a county borough. (Applause.) There is scarcely a town more fully equipped in municipal undertakings than Warrington, yet we are all aware that there is still much to be done in the widening of its main streets, the extension of its tramways and electric lighting, and better means of access between the parts severed by the Mersey. I have, as you are aware, taken an active • part in an endeavour to widen Warrington Bridge and I hope the Council will soon determine whether this improvement should be effected by means of canti- levers or a new bridge. As we all know, there is great need for a new bridge across the Mersey at Howley, giving access to 'Victoria Park, and which will be a reat boon to the people, especially the children, living g in the Howley district. The benefit of such a bridge can readily be realised when you consider that it would save a mile and a half as between Black Bear Bridge and the Parish Church. lam also of opinion that a new bridge across the river diversion near the Stag Bridge would not only greatly relieve Wilderspool-causeway and Chester-road,but would tend to develop the whole of Arpley meadows for building purposes. You are aware that a new system of bridge construction has been perfected during the last few years and that good substantial bridges can be built by this method at about one-third the cost of an iron bridge. I must refer. but I do 40 with great reluctance, to the manner in which the proceedings of the CounMl have, during the last two or three years been conducted. A good deal of personal feeling has been unnecessarily intro- duced into the debates, and I express the earnest hope that the members of the Council should now endeavour to pull together with one accord so as to serve the hest interests of the town. T also hope that an earnest endeavour will be made to settle speedily the two or three outstanding disputes which have given rise to a good deal of personal rancour, and that when these matters are disposed of that the members of the Council will, with me, endeavour to conduct the business of the town with better feeling, and that we should join together in furthering the interests of the town in an efficient and at the same time economical manner. armin thank you, gentlemen, for your kindness, if it is a kindness, in electing me to the office of Mayor. I can only promise that with God's help and your assistance I will endeavour to conduct the business of this Council in a fair and impartial manner, (Applauss).

THE DEPUTY MAYOR.

The Mayor then nominated Alderman Bolton to act as his deputy during the ensuing year.

- - MAYOR'S CHAPLAIN. The Mayor appointed the Rel.t. William Bracecamp, vicar of St. Paul's, as his chaplain.

ALDERMEN AND CONIMMES.

The next business was the adoption of the Standing Orders for the regulation of the proceedings of the Council and for the government of-committees. Alderman TINNION poved an amendment to the Standing Order dealirtil with the constitution of the committees,to the effect that a new regulation that not more than three aldermen be elected on one committee, be deleted. It might be said that he objected because he was an alderman. (Councillor PEMBERTON : Hear, hear.) He knew someone would say" Hear, hear "and commit themselves. (Laughter.) He objected to the alteration on principle—a word which was often misused in that Council—and if he had only been an ordinary Councillor he would have objected. Every member, whether a councillor or an alderman, had a perfect right to sit on as many committees as he could with advantage to the Council devote his time and abilities to. The effect of the new Sanding orders had been a sweeping change, and he said deliberately that it had not been to the advantage of the composition of the various committees. Men had been knocked off simply because they were aldermen. For instance, Alderman Pierpoint was removed from committees wholesale, and the ex-Mayor and others were similarly treated.

- Alderman BENNETT seconded. He had the mis- fortune to be an alderman—at least he was inclined to regard it as a misfortune, because the fact of holding the position exposed one to taunts which e- times undeserved. He considered that the adoption of the new standing order would be a very serious mistake. Only one principle ought to be present in their minds, and that was as to whether the men selected for the committees were the most qualified to render efficient service. Whatever their other claims or abilities might be, the aldermen had more experience than the other members of the Council and that was certainly an important factor in the successful management of rauniciral undertakings. Then they were also able to look at things with a certain amount of impartiality which was not always possible to a Councillor. He believed in democratic government and in obeying the wishes of the people, but the wishes of the people were sometimes only partially and temporarily expressed through cliques or in the mere heat of the moment. The member who had to rely for his position upon votes might not always be capable of looking at matters with the impartiality that was possible to an Alderman. Much had been said as to the necessity of aldermen going before the ratepayers periodically, and he con- sidered that while there was a great deal to be said for that course there were also arguments on the other side. Councillor Dr. JosEett agreed that every man should be taken on his merits in electing the committees. He did not, however, concur with Alderman Bennett that Councillors were more apt to be mere puppets of public opinion and pander to the wishes of constituents. He for one, and he thought many other Councillors, would assert their independence of mind and opinion as long as they felt they were doing right. Councillor LORD said he was responsible for the standing order and he considered that ample provision had been made for the aldermen on the committees. Considering that only nine of the thirty-six members of the Council were aldermen, he thought three was a very fair representation. Larger towns than Warring- ton had adopted a similar rule and found it to work well. There had been some awful blundering and mis- management in the past. fre was not going to blame the aldermen for it—he did not know whether they were altogether to blame. Alderman TINNION : I repudiate it as an Alderman, absolutely. Councillor PEMBERTON : Oh, you will do. Councillor LORD : It does not redound to the credit of some of the past governors of Warrington,and I shall stand by the Standing Order as proposed. Councillor PEMBERTON pointed out that even the House of Commons had to bow to public control and there was not the slightest doubt that psblic opinion was tending in the direction of doing away with Alder- men altogether. Aldermen had not been elected by merit in the past. Alderman TINNION : Some of them. (Laughter). ' Councillor PEMBERTON said it did not follow that because a man was an Alderman he was the best fitted for committee work. Judging by their attendances, it would appear that some Aldermen did not care about being on committees. They had more than a fourth of representation and that ought to be quite sufficient. It did not follow that a new-comer to the Council would be the biggest dunce in municipal matters, but yet it was the custom to place new members on such com- mittees as the Fire Engine Committee or Cemetery Committee. (Laughter.) Continuing, he said the attendances were the great factor in deciding who should be struck off the committees. Alderman Bolton supported the amendment. The purpose of the new standing order was to unseat some members who knew more of the work than the men who wanted to replace them. (" Question.") The standing order acted unfairly for the important committees on which the Alderman were placed were generally served by those who understood the work best. Did Coun- cillor Pemberton say that his (Alderman Bolton's) attendances were not satisfactory ? Councillor PEMBERTON. I did not mention you specifically. Alderman BOLTON: My attendances on the Watch Committee are greater than anyone else's,and on several other committees also. So far as my position is con- cerned it means that I shall simply have to attend here about half an hour a month on the Rivers Committee and an hour on the Town Hall Committee. Do you think that is sufficient for the services I ought to render the town, if I want to do more? Continuing Alderman Bolton contended that the reason for his exclusion from committees was so that he could not supervise things. He had seen members who had gone there ffora spleen and had kept the committee waiting for an hour simply because they did not agree with what was being done. He hoped the Council would rise above cliques and say distinctly that men who had served them well ought to be trusted again. Both Alderman Pierpoint and Alderman Fairclough who had been struck off some committees, had worked very hard. The Aldermen took the lead and bore the brunt of the battle and he objected to the "tail enders " trying to lead the Council and to sweep men off the committees who could criticise them and watch their actions. Councillor STOTT supported the amendment. Whilst he agreed with the principle of the resolution he thought it went too far. He would agree to not more than four Aldermen being on any one committee. It seemed to him that to take old members of 16 or 17 years standing off important committees and replace them by new members of the Council was hardly wise as the new members could not possibly be a.s qualified as those who had had great experience. Councillor TooLE and Councillor FULTON SMITH also supported the amendment. Councillor EVANS said that whilst the resolution was hardly the wisest they could adopt, he thought some sensible decision could be come to, and therefore he supported it at the caucus meeting. He thought they must have the fittest man for the fittest place, irrespec- tive of whether he was an Alderman or not, if the efficiency of the Council was to be maintained. Councillor BaLL objected to the minute as being unnecessary. It was quite open to tha Council to change the constitution of the committees at any time by refus- ing to elect whoever they wanted to leave off. The voting resulted as follows : FOR THE AMENDMENT. AGAINST. The Mayor._

Aldermen Bolton. Alderman Pierpoint. Bennett, Councillors Evans. Francomb. ft Barton. Tinniou. ft Skelland. If Evans, Ashcroft. Forshaw. Wilding, Councillors Clegg. PI Dickinson. Toole. Cunnington. Wilkinson. Lord. .• Fulton Smith. Pemberton. Neild. Benson-11. Sankey. Ellison. Duncan. Dr. Joseph. • Steel. 9, Wilson-19. Majority S. Councillor LORD then moved as a further amendment that the standing order read 4 Aldermen instead of 3. Councillor WILDING seconded. The amendment was defeated by 19 votes to 14. Councillor SrorT moved as an addition to the Standing Order regulating the filling af casual vacancies on committees " That any member of the Council may move an amendment to substitute the name of another member of the Couneil for the one recommended." His motion was, he said, with the object of preventing the Chairman or two or three members of a committee filling vacancies which might arise from time to time. It had been said that some committees were family concerns and that the Chairman and Deputy Chairman made selections from the Council not from the stand- I point of experience and ability. Alderman TiTsioN seconded. He thought it desirable for everyone to have a voice in the appointments. Councillor LORD agreed with the amendment though he thought that all vacancies should be notified to the Council and filled by the Council. The amendment found a strong 'supporter in Coun- cillor Dr. Joseph who complained that he was a mem- ber of the Council for six months before he was put upon any Committee, fie thought there was .something wrong when such as that was possible. The amendment was approved. Alderman TINSION criticised the Standing Order that in future printed copies of the minutes be given only to the members of the Council and the officials. He did not think that wise. The newspapers would not get the agenda paper and would have to fish. Some already knew how to fish whilst others could learn. It was felt, however, that the proposal should be given a trial, and it was approved. THE CONSTITUTION OF COMNIITTEES. The Council then proceeded to elect the varions corn= mittees. "THE ENEMY'S CAMP."

(By Sheringham and Meakin : Macmillan and C0.,85.). Quite oae of,the most entertaining and amusing stories we have read for some time is "The Enemy's Camp," by Hugh Sheringlaam and Nevill Meakin. The joys of camping out on a quiet English river inspire much of the atmosphere of this story while the eternal war of the sexes supplies a great deal of its humour. It is a book for those who love laughter and appreciate the open air. The complications that result from the proximity of a bachelor's house-boat, ,1 and " The Enemy's Camp," whose inhabitants are young, fair, and feminine, give rise to many amusing situations, and there is plenty of opportunity for wholesome fun. Naturally the habitues of houseboat and camp get somewhat intermingled as the story proceeds, and the reader's chief regret will be that unkind Fate has neglected to provide an equal number of both sexes in the rival camps. There are five , young men and only three maidens, so that two of the former are eventually left somewhere in the outer darkness after experiencing milder adventures of their own. One of the best features of the story is the ease and naturalness of the writing. Dialogue and descriptive matter are alike capital. The char- acters frequently indulge in witticisms, but they are of so spontaneous and bright a character that they never pall on the reader, who will find all the parties to the story eminently human, understandable people. "The Enemy's Camp "is a book which should appeal equally to members of both sexes, and with the Christmas season ppproaching this fact is probably worth noting.

===== 0002601_19061110_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
"A LADY OF ROME."

(By F. Marion Crawford : Macmillan and Co. Gs.) Mr. F. Marion Crawford is keeping very faithful t 3 Rome for the scene of his novels, and the last three from his pea have all been pitched in the " eternal city." There is probably no one so well qualified as Mr. Crawford to write fascinating fiction of Rome and the Romans, but the book-reader is apt to be some- what exacting, and a little more variety in the fare provided would, we think, be advisable. It would be more excusable of the author to live in and write of his favourite city continually if he maintained the high standard of his earlier novels, but we are bound to say that " A Lady of Rome " falls a long way below the quality of " Cigarette-maker's Romance, Katherine Lauderdale," " Casa Braccio," and " Mr. Isaacs," to mention just a few of Mr. Crawford's many famous novels. There is all his charm of manner and fascinating characterisation in " A Lady of Rome," but the plot is thin—such as exists —and not worthy of Mr. Crawford's ingenuity. Why,for instance,should Bassaldare Castiglione, the hero, who is described as a soldier and a man of action more than a thinkel, suddenly solve—by acute reasoning or a process of intuition quite beyond even the reader who is in possession of nearly all the *facts,— what is the crux of a problem presented by the theft of the Countess of Montalto's illicit love-letters? There was no evidence of any value to connect the proprietor of a certain gambling-saloon with the attempted blackmail, yet Castiglione goes straight to him, presents a pistol to his head and obtains a signed document which saves the honour of the woman he loves. Now the affair is managed very neatly but the author has left rather too much to the Imagination of the reader instead of conducting him skilfully along the path leading to elucidation. The denounement as thus a trifle inartistic -an unusual fault for Mr. Craw- ford to be guilty of. Maria Montalto's part in the story is that of a woman who loved not wisely but too well, and haii to confess,after an early rnariage de convenience that her child is not her lawful husband's. Then follow seven years' separation from both husband and lover, the return of both, and the convenient death of the former just when Maria's devotion to duty is almost at breaking point. The other characters in the story are very subsidiary, and after all there is not much to write nearly 400 pages about. We hope Mr. Crawford will shift his field of action before publishing another novel. He is too brilliant a writer to be allowed to slip into a rut without a reminder from his public.

"RUNNING HORSE INN." (By A. T. Sheppard: Macmillan and Co. Gs.)

We must confess to being much disappointed with Mr. Sheppard's new novel. The author of " The Red Cravat aroused anticipations which have not been realised, and "Running Horse Inn" is much inferior to its predecessor both in point of sustained interest and romantic flavour. The story gives a remarkably faithful picture of English life in the time of George IV., but the historical events whjch are interwoven are of little significance and do not lift the narrative out of the mediocre. Right from the commetmement the reader is somewhat misled. His sympathies are aroused on behalf of a young soldier returning from , the victorious campaign in Spain, and looking hope- fully to a union with the " girl he left behind him." "Running Horse Inn" is his old home and on arrival there with feelings softened by sentimental recollec- tions he is just in time to witnesA his brother, John , Kennett, return with a blushing bride who is none other than the lady of our young soldier's roseate dream. The reader feels a natural grudge against the stay-at-home brother, only to find as the tale proceeds that he is the real hero. John Kennett is rather a fine personalty, slow to act but honest and steadfast in purpose. His wife, Bess, makes a charming heroine. With such dsappoint- ments awaiting him, George naturally goes to the bad and mixes up with a revolutionary party in London whose sordid and silly insurrection fills a needless place in the book. The climax is ingenious and tragic, but the sad fate which o'ertakes both John and Bess is the culmination of the feeling of dissatis- faction which a perusal of the book has provoked. It was surely enough to have brought the luckless George to the scaffold, without tumbling poor Bess down a cliff and plunging her sore-tried husband into a despair which would probably end in insanitry or an early grave. We are sure the reader will feel that be could have made a much better job of the finale than the author who might have let a rather dull story end more brightly:

"JASPER." (A story for children, by Mrs. Molesworth : Macmillan and Co., 4s. 6d.)

Children will be delighted to learn that Nrs• 1 Molesworth has written a new story specially for them, and if this catches the eye of their parents they should feel in duty hound to purchase " Jasper " at once. It is quite in Mrs. Molesworth's best and brighest vein, and with charming illustrations by Gertrude Hammond should make an ideal Christmas or birthday book for the youngsters.

THE CRANFORD SERIES.

(" Scenes of Clerical Life," illustrated by Hugh

Thomson Macmillan and Co., Gs.)

. , The most recent publication in the Cranford Series is George Eliot's Scenes of Clerical Life." The story needs no recommendation, of course, but refer- ence must be made to the beautiful illustrative work of Mr. Hugh Thomson, with which it is embellished. Scarcely an incident but what has been reproduced in line by Mr. Thomsores facile pencil. Sixteen of the illustrations are printed in colour and are veritable works of art. Like all editions in the Cranford series, the book is beautifully printed and artistically bound. It will make a handsome addition to any up-to-date library. The drawings alone are worth more than the purchase price.

the continued and well-deserved popularity of Egypt as a Winter Resort. The Cairo correspondent who writes on" The Winter Season in Egypt " gives a bright picture of Society life in and around the City of the Caliphs, and notifies the most recent changers designed for the comfort of the season resident and for the occasional visitor. In another article the sportmau obtains a brief outline of the attractions which Egypt can offer to him, and the facts that the Khedive is now the possessor of a fine turbine steam yacht, that the Nile is now possible to motor-boats, and that a motor-road 100 miles in length is in contemplation, show that,with all its antiquarian associations, Egypt is very modern in its sport. The visitor who is new to the land is not forgotten, and a sell-known writer on travel subjects gives him many valuable hints on his journey, and On the various methods of " doing "Egypt in comfort. "THE CROWN." This popular illustrated paper has recently been sold. A new volume began last month under new auspices as a private property. \ It is to retain all the features that distinguished it from other illustrated weekly papers and some that were contemplated but never carried out "under the old regime, the principal being a development of the art of colour printing for which extensive arrangements have been made. It is announced that at least four to eight faithful reproductions in colour will ' appear week by week. RATIONALIST PRESS PUBLICATIONS. Messrs. Watts are publishing, for the Rationalist Press Association, a selection from Hume, in the form of a sixpenny reprint. It comprises the two important essays, "An Inquiry Concerning Human Understanding" and "An Inquiry Concerning the Principles of Morals." Together with the above there is published a new popular edition, in the R.P.A. Extra Series, of Mr. Chilperic Edwards's version of the Hamruurabi Code. under the title of "The Oldest Laws in the World." The same publishers are also issuing two other six- pennies. In the first, under the title of " A Few Footprints," Mr. J. Passmore Edwards, the well-known philanthropist, furnishes some interesting auto- biographical chapters. Mr. Edwards, during his varied career, has met several celebrities, including Thomas Carlyle. and he recalls many noteworthy incidents in I which they figured. The other sixpenny deals with " Socialism : Its Fallacies and Dangers," and is a trenchant ' analysis of the policy which at present I appears to be uppermost in the counsels of the Labour Party. The brochure is contributed to by some of the ablest writers in the Individualist movement, and it is claimed that the various arguments are supported by facts and figures which it will be difficult to gainsay. Messrs. Watts are also issuing a work on "Christianity and Tradition," by a new author, Mr. P. G. Blyth. Its point of view is that of the man whose interests are practical, not academic. It is written for those who, while they find in Christianity much that they value, flndalso in the forms in which Christianity is presented to thorn much that is untelligible, improbable, or unreasonable. It concludes that the teaching of Jesus, as related in the Gospels, is very different from that of most Christian tnachers, being in full accord with modern and scientific thought. The struggle of Rationalism, it is contended, is not with Christ, but A with traditions about him SHAKESPEARE'S REVENGE ON SIR THOMAS LUCY. I need but recall to your recollection the famous I scene at the opening of "The Merry Wives of Windsor," where Justice Shallow enters in a great fury of indigna- tion against Falstaff for breaking his park and stealing the deer, thereby abusing in his person a very ancient family .whose members for three hundred years had signed themselves " armigero," and " borne the dozen white luces in their coat." Upon which the kindly Welsh parson, Sir Hugh Evans, misunderstanding the kind of limes referred to—for a lune was the fish generally called a pike—and also mistaking the nature of the "coat "on which they figured, remarks : The dozen white louses do become an old coat well. Now the pun in itself is so poor that it is incon- ceivable Shakespeare introduced if for its own sake ; and when we know that no other family but that of Sir Thomas Lucy bore this charge of the lune, and they bore it in reference to their name, it is put beyond doubt that Shakespeare intended a personal affront ; while by substituting twelve luces for three,which was the number on the Lucy coat, he kept on the windy side of the Star Chamber. We cannot pretend to judge Shakespeare in this matter, because we do not know the extent of the provocation he had received. Tradition says be was " whipt." Speaking for myself, I cannot be sorry that his resentment took this shape, because it has supplied me, times without number, with an unanswerable question to' put to those persons who tell one that Shakespear's plays were written by Bacon : viz., bow Bacon, who was a friend and correspondent of Sir Thomas Lucy's, can be conceived making this unprovoked and very ungentlemanlike jest upon another gentleman's coat of arms ? Shakespeare at the date of the" Merry Wives of Windsor," was not yet " a gent], man born."—From "Shakespeare," in the " Cor-* Magazine "for November. TMR. WILFRID BLUNT'S ARABS To-day Mr. Blunt may claim tiat most of the Arabs in the world have passed through his stud. E where, the sultan of Muscat is still the owner of a siderable stud, and there are a fewsurvivors of powerful Princes of Arabia who are masters horses ; hut Mr. Blunt is of opinion that there -less than 3,000 thoroughbred brood mares left in Arabia to-day, and perhaps 5,000 the world over. He has pointed out that the special object of the Bedouin in breeding the Arab was service in war. raor, this purpose endurance is the chief thing, and the Al.lO. even when imported into England, has an iron c otifotah.o English sqhu winter.r s very Alittle shelter, e er, Buildingseven n s ot hat' stio 1 ‘worster y old barns that seem to have ventilation holes in every beam serve for winter quarters in the very severe weather. Neither sun nor wind can hurt the Arab, his temper is of the best, and his capacity for travelling seems to know no limits. For many years the annual sale of Arabs at Crabbet Park was one of the social events of July, but now the I horses are sold privately, the stud being too well-known to require the support of a special function. The chief use to which the Arab is put in these days is to serve as sire to half-breed stocks raised for service under hard conditions and often in foreign lands. The fact that the Arab can make no show on the flat against the British thoroughbred has done a great deal to reduce the demand, although, of course the Arab is the father of the British racehorse, and, associated with the heavy animal that was in Great Britain when Julius Cmsar came over, has produced the thoroughbred. Mr. Blunt has remarked that the points of the best Arab to-day and those of the great English thoroughbred sires of a century ago vary but slightly. R. Crockett's charming new story, " Little ,sson." The selection of coloured cartoons consists of no fewer than twenty of these portraits of Great Explorers and Inventors." The School-boy discourses on " Football," and then bids his audience a quaint farewell ; and the story of Anthony ./iala's Arctic expedition is accompanied by the most successful photo- graphs of polar expedition ever yet brought home.

" ECE iromo;" by Sir J. R. Seeley, „ _ _

K.C.M.G., Litt. Sir J. R. Seeley published this book anonymously in octavo form as long ago as 1865. 'lt created a great controversy, which burned none the less vigorously because the author chose to remain unknown. It called forth " Ecce Deus," a reply from the versatile pen of the late Dr. Parker. No doubt it is necessarily incomplete as a survey of the life and work of Jesus. None the less it is a fine book, and remains one of the classics of our tongue.

"LOVE TRIUMPHANT." Mrs. L. T. Meade,'s new novel is entled "Love Triumphant," and is dedicated to the, late G. F. Watts, FLA. It is published by Messrs. T. Fisher Unwin at 4s. 6d.

BIBBI"S "ANNUAL." The fact that " Bibby's Quarterly " has now become " Bibby's Annual " will be a source of regret to very I many people for the simple reason that they can now only get this high-class magazine once a year instead of four times as formerly. Increasing responsibility has curtailed the time of Mr. Bibby,of cattle-cake fame, and he has been compelled to abandon the old familiar title and quarterly issue. The " Annual " which has just been issued from the press for the first time, seems to have compressed the many excellencies of four quarterlies into its beautifully printed pages. Both from a literary and artistic standpoint the magazine reaches a very high standard and should afford pleasure to a large number of subscribers. The contents are very numerous and varied, but special attention should be called to the four coloured reproductions of famous original paintings in the gallery of the Louvre at Paris. They are superb in tone-value and realism. The only thing we regret in this connection is that the reproduc- tions are " backed" by letterpress printing, which some- what detracts from the otherwise splendid finish of the work. The pictures are worthy of a special plate and page. We haven() other criticism to offer of the journal which is much more' than a good shilling's worth. 'THE FREE CHURCEIMAN." We have received an advance copy of the January number of the" Free Churchman," which the National Free Church Council specially produces for localisation. Despite the excellence of the former issues of this magazine, the January number seems to have established a fresh record ; it would be difilcult to conceive any- thing more suitable for the home and church. The publishers claim for the " Free' Churchman " an increased circulation during the last three or four years of 100,000 copies per issue, which assuredly is eloquent testimony to the' standard of excellence maintained. With the number is included the programme and arrangements for 1907, and a splendid Art Gallery of portraits in colours well worthy of framing. The con- tributors for 1907 include practically all the leading Free Church speakers and writers, and the programme is thoroughly varied and interesting. All who are con- cerned with localising will do well to write for a specimen copy to the Publishers, at the Memorial Hall, E.C.

THE FIRESIDE MAGAZINE. Again we welcome the newest and freshest of issues of that popular monthly the "Strand Magazine." From among the numerous articles we must mention first and foremost an interesting and well illustrated paper on Lloyds'," that mysterious and powerful institution of which the average reader hears so much and know so little. "A Day at the Zoo " is the second of a series of articles, each article thereof containing no less than one hundred pictures reproduced from photographs. The subject this month is " Our Zoo," and from an educational point of view, if nothing else, this series should meet with the greatest possible appreciation. " Choral Societies," profusely illustrated with portraits of famous conductors and groups of performers, includ- ing references to the Warrington Musical Society, and a portrait of Mr. F. 'H. Crossley, makes interesting reading; and " The Chronicles of the Strand Club " are as amusing as ever. "My Best Picture," illustrated from paintings selected by the most eminent American painters, gives some beautiful examples of transatlantic art ; and in "Some Novel Projectiles," Mr. Archibald Williams gives the results of some highly interesting experiments which he has made by means of paper, water and wooden projectiles from an ordinary sport- ing gun. Fiction, for which the " Strand" is now so famous, is represented by a capital instalment of " Sir Nigel," by A. Conan Doyle, and half a dozen excellent short stories by tho foremost short story writers of the day ; another story of the well-known " Some Further Experiences of an Irish R.M." by E.(E. Somerville and Main Ross being specially worthy of notice. When one remembers that this issue contains 120 literary pages and no less than 245 illustrations, one cannot but wonder Messrs. Newnes can supply this publication at the moderate price of 6d. THE NOVEMBER "GRAND." There are some capital features in the ever-interesting and ever-up-to-date • Grand Magazine." "How Physic is Faked "should be read and carefully pondered over bty everyone concerned either for his own safety or for that of a dea'i• one ; many an aching regret might thus be avoided. Of no less interest is the powerful and eloquent pleading of that champion of English common sense, George R. Sims, in favour of the industrious lower middle class,which is now being sacrificed whole- sale to satisfy the insatiable maw of what " Dagonet " pithily describes as " The Moloch of the Rates," an idol worshipped nowadays by far too many of our spend- thrift municipal bodies. In " Society Marriage Broking " me have another thoroughly topical discussion which shows very unpleasant sidelights upon some of the practices winked at, if not openly tolerated by the class which should set an example of uprightness to the less fortunate sections of the community. It is with positive relief we turn to a delicious collection of "Irish Humorous Stories," any one of which will bear quoting, and any one of which may be relied upon to seta table iq a roar. But we are still not at the end of our catalogte, for we find, on turning over the pages of the "Grand" again, that we have made no mention of half a dozen other articles just as interesting as any of those re- ferred to,notably, "What is a Cold ?"by Dr. Robert Bell ; and " The Secret of Success in Literature," comprising the recipes of a score of the best-known living authors, as well as a contribution—one of the last things she penned—of that gifted woman, " John Oliver Hobbes." Nor, with all this wealth of articles have the tastes of lovers of fiction been overlootred. In addition to a very long instalment of the "Botor Chaperon," by those master hands, C. N.and A. M. Williamson, there are no less than three first-rate short stories. "A TREASURE-HUNT IN THE Aacnc." The November "Wide World Maigine" makes an excellent fireside companion with its teen stories and articles of first-class interest. One of the best narratives in the number is "Father Raymond's Burglar," a strange story told by a missionary clergy- man, but " A Treasure-Hunt lathe Arctic," a fascinating record of adventure in the frozen north, runs it very close. Mr. R. L. Jefferson furnishes the first article of an exclusively written series dealing with his recent record motor trip from London to Constantinople—the first motor to enter the Turkish Empire. Other most striking contributions are " My Friend the Leopard," the story of a battle of wits between a man and a man- eater; "Stealing a Secret," and "The Ghost of Musket Flat." A real-life detective story which holds one to the last line is "Tracking the Wire-Thieves," an Amerian secret service agent's narrative. Half a dozen other equally striking articles and stories make up a very strong number of this ever-welcome periodical, whose boast is that it is quite unlike any other magazine. "SUNDAY STRAND." A very striking article in the November" Sunday Strand "is entitled " Some Portraits of Christ as Con- ceived by American Artists." The article tells us that ten famous American artists were commissioned to show by their brush what their thoughts were concern- ing the bodily form of our Saviour, and this article shows the result, and a very fine result, it is. The pictures are splendidly reproduced. The fiction in this number is remarkably good. Mrs. L. B. Walford, the well-known novelist, writes a story entitled " The One too Many." Elizabeth M. Moon contributes " The Temptress " : Constance E, Thomas, Marguerite. A Winter Idyll " ,• and Horace Seymour Keller, " Big i Jim and Little Jim." There is certainly no religious magazine in the world which rivals the " Sunday Strand," either in variety or quality of its contents. It is always a credit to its publishers and its editor.

Ivtent Esson.

As a public lecturer the Rev. Arthur Mursell, of London, enjoys a high reputation and there was a good attendance at Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Tuesday levening when this veteran minister delivered a lecture lon his 50 years experiences on both sides of the Border, Channel, and Atlantic. Mr. Mursell, who laboured with great success in Manchester from 1857 to 1866, has just celebrated his ministerial jubilee, and among those who joined in the congratulatory meetings were Dr. McLaren, Dr. Robertson Nichol!, the Rev. Charles Williams, and Sir Thomas Fuller. Many Warrington people 'retain very happy recollections of Mr. Mursell's previous visits to this town and his lecture on Tuesday evening was looked forward to with considerable interest and pleasure.--Mr. James Dodds presided and said Mr. Mursell needed no introduction to a Warrington audience. He (Mr. Dodds) felt he must be getting an old man because ho was one of those who heard Mr. Mursell lecture • in Manchester on several occasions in the sixties, when as a boy be lived at Bolton and he went over to Manchester to hear Mr. Mursell speak in the Free Trade Hail Apparently Mr. Mursell was now as young and strong at ever and they hoped God would spare his life to the Church and to the country in which he was so highly esteemed, for these were days in which we wanted men' ..' who could show us what good men really were. (Applause). Mr. Mursell proceeded with his lecture which proved highly entertaining. 411. A GUARANTEED CURB FOR PILES, Itching, Blind, Bleeding, or Protruding. appeared to have the Wigan line at his mercy until Lapping saved finely. The visitors continued the pres- sure, and Wigan being penalized, Knight failed at goal. Volsey removed play Into the Warrington half by a clever a, and Topping and Jones had a chance of gettirrg to the line' but the ball was bungled. Voisey made an attempt to run in at the corner, but was brought down near the line. Subsequently, however, Landor and Cartwright endangered the Wigan line, and enabled Foster to score brilliantly. Knight landed the goal. Half-time, Warrington "A" five points, Wigan " A " nothing. Wigan attacked vigoronsly, and scrummaging ensued close to the visitors' line. Here the Warringtonians were penalized, and Voisey landed a goal. Immediately afterwards Knight had a penalty kick close to the posts, but he failed to register the goal. From some loose play Barker scored for Wigan, Voisey registering a goal. Result: Wigan "A" two goals one try (seven points), Warrington " A " one goal one try (five points). G. T. P. G. T. P .
